Yves here. Popular misperceptions of how Federal spending works, often promoted by the capitalist/creditor classes for their benefit, seem to require regular debunking. Hence the clear, compact entry by Tom Neuburger below.
By Thomas Neuburger. Originally published at God’s Spies
Scrooge McDuck, a kindlier billionaire. Artist: Mark Crilley, 2006.
This is Part 4 of a brief series on the U.S. dollar: What they are, where they come from, and why people are so confused about debts and deficits. Today we’ll reveal what the federal bond market is.
Other pieces in this series:
• Part 1 is about the dollar itself: “Why ‘Entitlement’ Cuts May Succeed”
• Part 2 explains why taxes are levied: “Why Governments Tax: A List”
• Part 3 discusses the ‘deficit’: “What Is This ‘Deficit’ We Hear So Much About?”
And Part 5, yet to come, will answer the question: If all of that’s true, then what is the national debt?
Onward. What is this “bond market” we’re told to so worry about?
Dollars: Our Story So Far
Let’s start with what we know from before:
Rule 1. Only our government makes dollars.
You can’t make dollars, not real ones, not ones you can spend. Every dollar in your pocket, and every dollar in every account you own, ultimately came from the government. That’s just a fact.
Rule 2. As a result, the government doesn’t need taxes in order to spend. Period. End of.
There are no ‘buts’ or ‘ands’ to this. Everyone you see on TV knows this is true. Every person who says “we don’t want the government printing money” acknowledges that indeed, the government, broadly considered, prints money. Again, there’s no other source of dollars but the U.S. government. (Yes, banks create dollars by lending and the Federal Reserve issues bills, but only by law, by government authorization, and under strict rules.)
Rule 3. Because government gets dollars from itself, the “deficit” isn’t a debt at all — it’s a gift.
If the country were started from scratch and dollars were king, but in its wisdom the government spent nothing at all, then hell, we’d all be broke. Government spending puts dollars in circulation. Period. End of. The so-called “deficit,” that so-scary word, is just additional dollars in circulation. And nothing more. The word “deficit” is a construct. It’s deceptive labeling. For how the construct is constructed, skip down to here.
So if government makes its own money, what’s the bond market? Isn’t it government borrowing? Inherently, no.
Rule 4. The selling of U.S. Treasuries is entirely a choice.
This follows from what’s above. If the government doesn’t have to borrow in order to spend, the Treasuries market must serve other goals. Let’s take a look.
What Is The U.S. Bond Market Really?
The “bond market” for these purposes means the market for U.S. Treasuries, called bills, notes or bonds depending on when they “mature”, when they can be redeemed.
Treasury bonds look like a loan — the government gets dollars from us, just like companies do, and pays interest till the bonds are redeemed at maturity. But because our government can make dollars out of thin air, it doesn’t actually need yours. So why does the bond market exist? It has several goals.
Bond Market Goal 1: A Way to Manage the Money Supply
Economist Stephanie Kelton explains this point in a piece in which she counters a statement Paul Krugman made about the relationship between deficits and interest rates. The details of that discussion aren’t important here. But in the piece she says:
The government [coordinates] its deficit spending with bond sales, thereby doing a reserve drain (selling bonds) along with a reserve add (deficit spending), so that the newly injected reserves are quickly transformed into newly added Treasuries. The bond sales are done to coordinate the impact so that the government’s fiscal operations don’t leave the banking system with a larger monetary base…. 
Kelton elsewhere repeats this:
[T]he sale of government bonds by the sovereign [is] something quite different from borrowing: bond sales are part of monetary policy and help the central bank to manage interest rates. Government’s [sic] don’t need to borrow their own currency! 
Obviously true, right? Deficits inject money into the system. Bond sales take money out, park that money in a bank called the U.S. Federal Reserve, and keep it from causing inflation. The interest is your reward for taking the deal.
So that’s Goal One of the Treasuries market — manage the money supply.
Bond Market Goal 2: Provide a Safe Haven for Dollars
What’s the second goal of the Treasuries market? If you think hard, you’ll probably guess it. When are Treasuries most bought? Normally, when times look tough and other investments look too risky. Treasuries serve as a risk-free safe haven for money. Gold serves much the same function, though it’s less risk-free.
And since the U.S. government is (up to this moment at least) the king of the world, the dollar is king of money, and U.S. bonds are the safest of all investments. That benefits everyone with dollars to invest. But it benefits most those with the biggest pile. I’ll say it again, the Treasury market’s a gift to those with much cash.
Side Note — Why Are Treasury Prices Down Now?
Why is that not true today — why are Treasury prices falling even though global risks seem especially high? These are not normal times, and gold’s not immune.
Gold hasn’t been immune [from price drops]. Data suggests bullion and gold ETFs such as $GLD experienced selling pressure even as headlines worsened. The reason is pragmatic: margin calls, rebalancing, and forced liquidity needs push traders to sell highly liquid assets first — and gold and Treasuries are prime candidates. The numbers point to a liquidity-driven dynamic rather than a pure risk-on rotation; in short, investors are prioritizing cash over protection. 
This is the last five years of the gold market:

The peak in the chart occurred on February 23, a week before the U.S. attacked Iran. What the outcome of that war will be is obvious to anyone with eyes:

The U.S. will lose its empire, its one-power status. Goodbye to the king.
If the Iran war continues and Gulf petroleum stays scarce, the world could see a real, global, energy-driven depression that lasts for years. That will hurt everything.

End of side note — as you can see, this is a whole other topic.
What Ties Treasury Bonds to Government Spending?
If the creation of Treasury bonds is voluntary — if our government has no need to borrow what it can create — why do people think its bonds are a government debt?
Answer: Because our government passed a series of laws — again, voluntarily — that ties some of its spending to the selling of bonds. This was a choice.
If you care, here’s how those laws work:

The U.S. Constitution requires all government spending to be approved by Congress (Art. I, § 9, cl. 7). Note: Congress can authorize spending without raising revenue. This is a key point.
The Antideficiency Act (31 U.S.C. § 1341) forbids the government from spending money that hasn’t been appropriated. They can spend cash on hand, but only up to what Congress allows. The Act was first passed in 1870 to curb post-Civil War government spending.

This allows unlimited spending, which happens today. Now the constraints:

A law governing the issuance of U.S. Treasury notes — 31 U.S.C. § 5115 — limits the Treasury Department’s ability to literally print money, paper bills. This in turn limits its cash on hand. That law allows the Treasury to issue “United States currency notes,” but caps the total at $300,000,000, a pittance today. In addition, those Treasury notes “may not be held or used for a reserve”. (This is another leftover from the Civil War era, when the Legal Tender Act of 1862 allowed the government to issue paper money not backed by gold, the so-called “greenbacks”. Remember, this is the “gold standard” era. We’re not tied to gold any more.)
If the Treasury cannot print paper money on its own, who can? The Federal Reserve Act, Section 16 grants the Federal Reserve bank the right to do that. That’s why the bills in your pocket say “Federal Reserve Note” and not something like “U.S. Treasury Note.” The Federal Reserve Act was first passed in 1913 under Woodrow Wilson.
Finally, the Federal Reserve Act, Section 14(b) forbids the Fed from financing the government directly. When it runs low on cash, the Treasury Department is required to sell bonds on the open market, bonds that anyone can buy, including the Fed. “[A]ny bonds, notes, or other obligations which are direct obligations of the United States … may be bought and sold without regard to maturities but only in the open market” (emphasis mine). Without that requirement, the Fed could just hand over cash and the Executive Branch could spend it.

And that, ladies and gents, ties government spending to bonds. A choice, and only a choice. There’s nothing in nature that makes things work this way.
Congress can authorize spending as much as it likes. The Treasury can spend as much cash as it has on hand, up to the limit of Congressional allocations. But when the Treasury runs out of cash, it’s forced to sell bonds.
Which reveals a Goal Three of this complicated construction: It allows the rich who run everything to freely give cash to themselves (looking at you, Raytheon) while still pleading poverty (look, the debt!) when it comes to nice things for you (like railroads, Social Security and good Medicare).
See how that works?
Next Up
Next up, the last in our series, What is the national debt? That will be short, I promise. And when we’re done, you’ll know all you need to know about why you’re not rich.

This is Part 4 of a brief series on the U.S. dollar: What they are, where they come from, and why people are so confused about debts and deficits. Today we’ll reveal what the federal bond market is.
Other pieces in this series:
• Part 1 is about the dollar itself: “Why ‘Entitlement’ Cuts May Succeed”
• Part 2 explains why taxes are levied: “Why Governments Tax: A List”
• Part 3 discusses the ‘deficit’: “What Is This ‘Deficit’ We Hear So Much About?”
And Part 5, yet to come, will answer the question: If all of that’s true, then what is the national debt?
Onward. What is this “bond market” we’re told to so worry about?
Dollars: Our Story So Far
Let’s start with what we know from before:
Rule 1. Only our government makes dollars.
You can’t make dollars, not real ones, not ones you can spend. Every dollar in your pocket, and every dollar in every account you own, ultimately came from the government. That’s just a fact.
Rule 2. As a result, the government doesn’t need taxes in order to spend. Period. End of.
There are no ‘buts’ or ‘ands’ to this. Everyone you see on TV knows this is true. Every person who says “we don’t want the government printing money” acknowledges that indeed, the government, broadly considered, prints money. Again, there’s no other source of dollars but the U.S. government. (Yes, banks create dollars by lending and the Federal Reserve issues bills, but only by law, by government authorization, and under strict rules.)
Rule 3. Because government gets dollars from itself, the “deficit” isn’t a debt at all — it’s a gift.
If the country were started from scratch and dollars were king, but in its wisdom the government spent nothing at all, then hell, we’d all be broke. Government spending puts dollars in circulation. Period. End of. The so-called “deficit,” that so-scary word, is just additional dollars in circulation. And nothing more. The word “deficit” is a construct. It’s deceptive labeling. For how the construct is constructed, skip down to here.
So if government makes its own money, what’s the bond market? Isn’t it government borrowing? Inherently, no.
Rule 4. The selling of U.S. Treasuries is entirely a choice.
This follows from what’s above. If the government doesn’t have to borrow in order to spend, the Treasuries market must serve other goals. Let’s take a look.
What Is The U.S. Bond Market Really?
The “bond market” for these purposes means the market for U.S. Treasuries, called bills, notes or bonds depending on when they “mature”, when they can be redeemed.
Treasury bonds look like a loan — the government gets dollars from us, just like companies do, and pays interest till the bonds are redeemed at maturity. But because our government can make dollars out of thin air, it doesn’t actually need yours. So why does the bond market exist? It has several goals.
Bond Market Goal 1: A Way to Manage the Money Supply
Economist Stephanie Kelton explains this point in a piece in which she counters a statement Paul Krugman made about the relationship between deficits and interest rates. The details of that discussion aren’t important here. But in the piece she says:
The government [coordinates] its deficit spending with bond sales, thereby doing a reserve drain (selling bonds) along with a reserve add (deficit spending), so that the newly injected reserves are quickly transformed into newly added Treasuries. The bond sales are done to coordinate the impact so that the government’s fiscal operations don’t leave the banking system with a larger monetary base….
Kelton elsewhere repeats this:
[T]he sale of government bonds by the sovereign [is] something quite different from borrowing: bond sales are part of monetary policy and help the central bank to manage interest rates. Government’s [sic] don’t need to borrow their own currency!
Obviously true, right? Deficits inject money into the system. Bond sales take money out, park that money in a bank called the U.S. Federal Reserve, and keep it from causing inflation. The interest is your reward for taking the deal.
So that’s Goal One of the Treasuries market — manage the money supply.
Bond Market Goal 2: Provide a Safe Haven for Dollars
What’s the second goal of the Treasuries market? If you think hard, you’ll probably guess it. When are Treasuries most bought? Normally, when times look tough and other investments look too risky. Treasuries serve as a risk-free safe haven for money. Gold serves much the same function, though it’s less risk-free.
And since the U.S. government is (up to this moment at least) the king of the world, the dollar is king of money, and U.S. bonds are the safest of all investments. That benefits everyone with dollars to invest. But it benefits most those with the biggest pile. I’ll say it again, the Treasury market’s a gift to those with much cash.
Side Note — Why Are Treasury Prices Down Now?
Why is that not true today — why are Treasury prices falling even though global risks seem especially high? These are not normal times, and gold’s not immune.
Gold hasn’t been immune [from price drops]. Data suggests bullion and gold ETFs such as $GLD experienced selling pressure even as headlines worsened. The reason is pragmatic: margin calls, rebalancing, and forced liquidity needs push traders to sell highly liquid assets first — and gold and Treasuries are prime candidates. The numbers point to a liquidity-driven dynamic rather than a pure risk-on rotation; in short, investors are prioritizing cash over protection.
This is the last five years of the gold market:

The peak in the chart occurred on February 23, a week before the U.S. attacked Iran. What the outcome of that war will be is obvious to anyone with eyes:
End of side note — as you can see, this is a whole other topic.
What Ties Treasury Bonds to Government Spending?
If the creation of Treasury bonds is voluntary — if our government has no need to borrow what it can create — why do people think its bonds are a government debt?
Answer: Because our government passed a series of laws — again, voluntarily — that ties some of its spending to the selling of bonds. This was a choice.
If you care, here’s how those laws work:
This allows unlimited spending, which happens today. Now the constraints:
And that, ladies and gents, ties government spending to bonds. A choice, and only a choice. There’s nothing in nature that makes things work this way.
Congress can authorize spending as much as it likes. The Treasury can spend as much cash as it has on hand, up to the limit of Congressional allocations. But when the Treasury runs out of cash, it’s forced to sell bonds.
Which reveals a Goal Three of this complicated construction: It allows the rich who run everything to freely give cash to themselves (looking at you, Raytheon) while still pleading poverty (look, the debt!) when it comes to nice things for you (like railroads, Social Security and good Medicare).
See how that works?
Next Up
Next up, the last in our series, What is the national debt? That will be short, I promise. And when we’re done, you’ll know all you need to know about why you’re not rich.

Deb, I understand. It’s counter-intuitive to the well-propagandized us. But just keep one thing in mind:
The U.S. government doesn’t need our dollars. It makes dollars from thin air.
Everything flows from that fact. Everything: what federal taxes are, Treasury bonds, the so-called deficit, the so-called national debt (which is really the totally optional Treasuries market).

Normally we say that to mean people should be frugal.  But take that away, and just consider that money is not a naturally occurring element, but instead it is a tool created by human beings that can be used for many functions.

For a monetary populist like myself the key issue is where various frameworks/theories locate monetary sovereignty.
MMT does not strictly ignore private bank money creation (Basil Moore’s horizontals).  But it layers a distinction on top: vertical money versus horizontal money.
Bank-created money is “horizontal”–every deposit matched by a loan, so it nets to zero across the private sector; only the currency issuing state, spending more than it taxes, creates “net financial assets” for the private sector. This tends to make money look more like mere private leverage and state money much more–the real thing.
So while MMT doesn’t ignore bank creation money, I see its architecture as systematically demoting it.
I believe it is fair to label MMT as chartalist to the bone: money is the creation of the state, taxes drive its acceptance, treasury and central bank are consolidated into one sovereign balance sheet, its policy program–deficit spending disciplined by inflation, and a federal jobs guarantee that runs entirely through the national budget.  It is a framework whose natural agent is the fiscal state.
For a monetary populist like myself, the dominant fact is that the overwhelming majority of the money supply is created by private banks at the point of lending, so the decisive political question becomes not the size of the deficit but the allocation of new credit–who gets it, for production or asset speculation, decided loan by loan.  Where MMT’s sovereignty is the treasury, I on the other hand tend to see the sovereigns as thousands of loan officers in small local banks, capable of potentially creating a populist producerism at the local/ county level.
Thank you.
“Let a thousand flowers bloom”
I suspect that part of the MMT focus on State monetary sovereignty is that State actors are better able to absorb risk and so have more policy freedom than do smaller and solvency-constrained actors such as private for-profit and not-for-profit lenders. 
A difficulty of the MMT focus on State fiscal policy is that this is of keen interest to other centers of power which, at present, have effective veto over the public-interest goals that MMT theorists typically would like to see pursued through the exercise of State fiscal power. It may be that your vision is more realistically achievable (or, perhaps better, less unachievable).
What MMT seems to neglect is that when a monetary sovereign creates money to spend it is also creating its own debt. For example a government has tax revenues of $1 trillion and spends $1.1 trillion, then it has deficit spending of $100 billion and a debt creation of $100 billion. The government goes, “What the hey! We created that money and spent if fair and square – how can you be such a downer and say we are in debt?” Quite simple actually, wherever the money was spent or whatever it was spent on, the economic actor that receives that money now has a claim on the country for the amount they hold. New, additional money – when spent creates a new additional liability. Note, when creditors hold money, they don’t earn interest – they just get to spend or invest it. They don’t have to buy from the originating country – especially in the case of the US which is a reserve currency, they can buy from anywhere in the world. 
According to classical monetary theory, an increase in the supply of money can lead to inflation, which would mean a general increase in prices with the real size of the economy unchanged. One of the claims of MMT used to be was that no longer held because the US didn’t see inflation until Covid. Well, that was not completely accurate because what was happening was that the US has been running consecutive trade deficits – since about 1973. So instead of generating inflation the US was going into debt with the rest of the world. Again, there is no return on holding cash, so what the country’s creditors did was buy government debt, real estate, corporate bonds and equities. These purchases increased demand for US dollars and kept the value of the dollar higher than it would normally be. What should actually happen when a country has a trade deficit is that its currency starts to fall, inflation is created, real wages fall and companies find it profitable to produce in the US again, thus reducing the trade deficit. But since US governments welcomed the offshoring of production, they wanted to keep the value of the dollar high and inflation low. Then as the economy slowed down (from over 4% in the 60s to about 2% since 2000), they cut taxes and kept pumping money into the economy to keep up appearances – every president in my recent memory has claimed that the economy under them has been the greatest ever and not one has supplied evidence to prove it. 
That leads to another challenge with MMT for me. The US economy is getting weaker with slower growth, higher deficits and stagnant standards of living for many Americans. Instead of calling for the need to change the economy, MMT people are saying that governments always have money for wars and tax cuts but they say no to the average person. With MMT, we can just create money to pay for your retirement, medicare and your wages if you can’t get a job – because there is an unlimited amount of money we can create. My heart is with the MMTers but my head doesn’t see it adding up. Creating and spending money is also creating debt, debt that grows and requires interest payments. I am pretty sure that Michael Hudson has shown us that out of control debt has been a hugely destabilizing feature in world economic history.
Government creates money out of nothing to also pay interest (assuming they didn’t do the stupid thing of borrowing in another currency). That’s why the interest is characterized as an upwards wealth transfer.
As for trade deficits, the way to think about it is that surplus countries, if they want to stay in surplus, have to recycle all the dollars. It can be among each other (everyone is accepting dollars), or back into the US markets/financial system which is the only place that can handle the scale involved, and also the “cleanest shirt in the laundry pile”.
Running trade deficits is tantamount to exporting jobs, and was seen as a way to put labor into its place, crushing unions, and at the same time pumping up corporate profits. It slows demand driven economic growth, but elites stay happy due to financialization.
As for debt, the question is it private or public debt? Mass media sure tries hard to conflate the two.
> With MMT, we can just create money to pay for your retirement, medicare and your wages if you can’t get a job – because there is an unlimited amount of money we can create.
The MMT understanding of the constraints on State fiscal policy are much more nuanced than this. Your characterisation ignores the reality of productive capacity constraints that is acknowledged by MMT — State fiscal policy is constrained by real limits on the economy’s ability to produce goods and services, not by limits on State money to pay for those. The problem of future retirement and medical care of a greying US population is going to be availability of labor and resources more than “ability to pay.”
Regarding growth, real physical output probably is going to slow, if not outright decline, worldwide in the not distant future, for the simple reason that the most easily accessible fossil energy and mineral resources have already been exploited and depleted. In future, the process of resource extraction will consume a larger fraction of global output than it has in the past. This may be a more disagreeable experience in US than in many other parts of the world, thanks to recent policy deprecating renewable energy in favor of fossil energy extraction.
 Yves has for years (probably decades) been arguing that radical conservation (with convergence of global living standards, and a decline in the standards in the West) is the only sustainable path forward. I think this is right. This is not a growth path.

51,2026-09-29,"Manus 2.0 is out, and its new Cue app gives each AI agent a phone, an email and a wallet. The launch comes five months after Beijing ordered Meta's $2bn deal for Manus cancelled.
Manus, the AI agent startup founded in China and now based in Singapore, launched Manus 2.0 on Monday. It also released Cue, a standalone app for personal AI agents. In Cue, each agent gets its own email address, phone number, wallet and computer, the company wrote in a blog post.
“Manus 2.0 is not a version update. It’s a new architecture, new products, and new capabilities,” Manus wrote.
The launch comes five months after Beijing ordered Meta to cancel its $2bn deal for the startup, as Micah Barkley reported for Bloomberg. Meta had agreed to buy Manus at the end of 2025.
Cue runs on the same infrastructure as Manus. Its agents can send messages and pay within a budget the user sets, according to Manus. Each can finish a task on its own machine. They can also take the user’s calls and leave a summary in the app.
Several agents can share a group chat with a common goal and hand work to each other. In the company’s example, one agent researches venues for a New York launch. Another makes a shortlist and a third drafts the deck. At a restaurant, a user can scan the QR code and have an agent order or hold a place in line.
Cue is in early access and free to use with an invite code. It works on the web, desktop and mobile, with iOS to follow after App Store review, Manus said.
The app arrives as Meta pushes its own personal agent. Meta launched Muse earlier this month, and it quickly climbed to the top of app store charts, Bloomberg reported.
Manus 2.0 runs on Cascade, the company’s new in-house system for running its agents. It brings in specialised tools only when a project needs them. In one tested setup, Cascade used 23.2% fewer tokens than the previous system, Manus said. It finished tasks in 28.2% less time and cost 32% less to run.
A new feature called Automations lets a task start when something happens in a connected service. That can be a new email, a Slack message, a calendar event or a Notion update. Until now, scheduled tasks could only start at a set time. Users can also buy a Cloud Computer, a dedicated environment that keeps running when their laptop is closed.
The desktop app becomes Manus Studio, a shared workspace for documents, spreadsheets, slides, websites, code, games and video. It adds two new environments. Video Editor gives users a timeline of the clips, images, text and audio Manus used, so they can swap pieces by hand. It is built for short product ads of 30 to 60 seconds, tutorials and vlogs.
Game Dev builds games that can be published as a website and played from a link. Its multiplayer servers run on a Cloud Computer.
“Manus empowers you to build. You decide what’s worth building,” the company wrote.
Remote Control lets users ask Manus from their phone to work on their own computer. They can watch the desktop live while it does. Manus first brought its agent to the desktop in March.
Manus, which split from Meta after China’s block, has said it is building a team to develop products for the Chinese market, Bloomberg reported. It is also in talks with local model makers and ecosystem partners.

53,2026-09-29,"Existing AI products may bring in $1.8tn a year at most by 2031, Bain says. The rest of the data centres' bill could come from ads, autonomy, robots and new products.
The AI industry will need about $6tn in annual revenue by 2031 to pay for the data centres being built today. Bain & Company makes the estimate in its Global Technology Report, published on Tuesday. Existing AI products could bring in only $1.2tn to $1.8tn of that.
“The debate today is fixated on employee productivity. The economics of AI infrastructure demand trillions in new revenue beyond productivity gains,” David Crawford, chairman of Bain’s global technology practice, said in a statement.
AI infrastructure is being built well ahead of demand, Crawford said. Funding it would require adding about 1% to the annual global GDP growth rate.
Annual spending on AI infrastructure could reach $1.5tn by 2031, the report says. That covers new data centres and compute, plus upgrades to the chips, memory and networking already installed.
Bain assumes capital spending runs at about 25% of industry revenue, in line with trends among cloud providers. At that ratio, the AI market would need to approach $6tn a year.
Microsoft, Google, Amazon, Meta and Oracle could spend $780bn on capital expenditure in 2026, Bain estimates. That is nearly five times the level of three years earlier. The largest AI data centres double in size and cost every 12 to 16 months or so, according to Epoch AI data in the report.
Consumer AI, through subscriptions and advertising, could bring in $200bn to $400bn by 2031. Enterprise AI could add $1tn to $1.4tn for providers, from software development, sales, marketing, customer service and IT. That leaves about $4.2tn still to find.
Bain names four sources for it. Chatbots that carry ads and replace much of web search could add $100bn to $200bn or more. Self-driving cars, trucks and drones, along with other industrial automation, are a $400bn opportunity.
Physical AI, meaning simulations, digital twins and robots, could be worth $900bn. That assumes a 10% cut in R&D and manufacturing costs. Products that do not exist yet could help close the gap. Bain gives AI-driven drug discovery, mental health support and new materials for batteries and chips as examples.
Bain’s data centre model projects $5tn to $6.5tn of spending on nearly 150 gigawatts of new capacity by 2030. That would almost triple global capacity in five years. Power, chips, skilled labour and permits are all short at the same time, the report says.
Local opposition blocked or delayed at least 75 projects worth $130bn in the first quarter, Bain says. That nearly matches the $156bn of projects disrupted in all of 2025. In July, New York became the first US state to pause giant data centres. Fourteen other states have introduced similar bills.
On Tuesday, OpenAI, Blackstone and US unions formed an alliance to head off state moratoriums. Earlier this month, PwC projected $31tn of AI infrastructure investment by 2050.
The report also finds that AI has cut a typical cyberattack from about four weeks to 18 hours.

by Peter O'Dowd, Here & Now's host-correspondent

                A large piece of ice that broke off the Mendenhall glacier in Juneau, Alaska.
                
                    
                    Peter O'Dowd/Here & Now
                    
                
hide caption

A flood happens like clockwork every summer in Juneau, Alaska.
When the weather warms up, a reservoir in the mountains above the city fills with billions of gallons of melted ice and eventually breaks past the glacial dam holding it back.
Even though I'm based in Arizona, I've been watching from afar every year since 2023, when the glacial outburst flood ripped houses into the raging Mendenhall River. The story is personal. My in-laws are from Juneau, and they live in the floodzone.
That's why it felt like it was time to bring Here & Now's climate-solutions project, Reverse Course, to the last frontier. While climate change is threatening lives and livelihoods around the globe, no state in the country has warmed faster than Alaska.
Along with fellow climate journalist Chris Bentley, we boarded small planes, ferries and helicopters to get a close-up view of the risks facing the state. Chris met people from the Chilkat Indian Village in Klukwan who are working to create a warning system for landslides triggered by heavy rain; and I spoke with Juneau residents racing to protect the city from glacial floods that are likely to last another 20 years, or more.
Thankfully, this summer, my in-law's house and the rest of Juneau stayed dry.
But about two weeks after we left town, a landslide triggered by a collapsing glacier ripped through mountain villages in Nepal and Tibet. While the scale of the devastation was beyond anything experienced in Alaska, the underlying truth remains: A warming planet is destabilizing ice sheets around the world. The hazards are getting closer to home.

56,2026-09-29,"Stockpiling and calls for military action are among signs of increasing precarity as economy conditions worsen.

Tehran, Iran – Iranians are stocking up on food and medicines as fears grow that the country will face dire shortages as the United States tightens an embargo on the country.
The Iranian government had managed to keep household staples flowing during the early months of the war. But a US naval blockade and pivot to draconian sanctions have led to fears that imports will be limited.
More recently, the US announced a ban on Iranian airlines worldwide and is pressing other countries to cut trade ties with Iran in a bid to isolate the country.
Addressing the United Nations General Assembly last week, Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian condemned Washington for its blockade on his country, saying it will prevent food and medicine from reaching the Iranian people.
On Saturday, US President Donald Trump rejected Iran’s offer for a seven-day truce that would see it reopen the Strait of Hormuz in exchange for economic relief and other conditions.
As the war enters its eighth month and hopes for a diplomatic solution dim, Al Jazeera looks at how some Iranians are faring under a US embargo.
 

 
Tehran’s stores remain stocked, but shoppers are keeping an eye on shop shelves. Their anxiety has deepened since a flurry of social media posts urging people to ensure they have several days of food before a possible new round of fighting with the US.
At one shopping centre, Ali, 51, was loading a trolley packed with rice, flour, pasta, pulses, cooking oil and canned food.
He is stocking up because he is convinced that Iran’s adversary is seeking to “starve” the people to push them to turn on their government.
“This is not a war in the traditional sense. It targets the people to put pressure on the authorities,” he said.
With flights to some nearby countries suspended, imports of certain medicines and mobile phone headsets are hard to find. The prices of those still available in shops have been pushed up.
 

 
Haj Pasandideh, a 71-year-old grocer, said he believed warehouses were well-stocked with basic goods at the moment. But the problem lies in the daily price increases due to Iran’s weakening rial, prompting him to bulk-buy goods before the expected hikes.
He sometimes caps cooking oil at one bottle per sale to ensure as many customers can purchase the goods as possible.
Farmers are also on edge due to the embargo, unable to ship out their produce and forced to sell to local markets.
The war has worsened the economic situation, even after years of sanctions that have pushed the country from being a regional exporter of agricultural and food products to being one of the region’s main importers.
Mehran, an apple grower, told Al Jazeera that due to the difficulties of exporting, he had sold only a third of this year’s harvest, all of it inside Iran.
Some neighbouring countries have halted trade with Iran in recent months, he said, while trucks now spend days at crossings they once cleared in a day, causing fruit to spoil before it reaches buyers.
“The blockade has not only closed the sea and air but also the land routes but in an undeclared manner,” he said.
 

 
The war has also deepened an already-dire shortage of medications. Some pharmacies Al Jazeera visited in southern Tehran had run out of infant formula and dozens of other types of medications.
Although officials have assured Iranians that substitute products were available, pharmacy staff expressed concern about the increasing number of customers searching for infant formula in vain.
“Imported medicines are disappearing fast, and lately people have been coming in to buy certain items before they need them for fear they will run out in future,” Mahsa, a 23-year-old pharmacy assistant, told Al Jazeera.
She said the health authorities had introduced “smart systems” to track the quantity and type of medication each person buys.
The prospect of a prolonged siege and deteriorating economic conditions have driven some Iranians to call for a military solution.
On Tehran’s main thoroughfare, several people told Al Jazeera that another round of fighting with the US was unavoidable. Some urged the government to break the blockade by force before it is tightened further.
Mohsen, a 23-year-old economics student, believes Washington’s goal is to strangle the Iranian economy and the cost will ultimately be felt by Iranian citizens.
Iran possesses many strong cards that it must use to break the air and sea blockade before they develop into a land blockade that would completely choke Tehran, he said.
Alireza Taghavinia, an Iranian international relations expert, told Al Jazeera that the gap between Washington and Tehran was too wide to be bridged. “The siege imposed by force will only be lifted by force,” he said.
In a worst-case scenario, falling living standards could bring protesters onto the streets, he added.
“The enemy is counting on stirring up chaos inside Iran because it believes no military strike will achieve its objectives without internal unrest,” he said.

57,2026-09-29,"When I decided to pursue a master's degree in the US, I was not simply looking for a good school. I had built up an idea of what studying there could mean for my career and, if I am being honest, for my life.
I applied to several universities, but Columbia University and New York were the dream I had absorbed from years of movies and television while growing up in Mexico.
The other schools felt like alternatives until the University of Pennsylvania unexpectedly offered me a $40,000 scholarship and forced me to ask how much that dream was actually worth.
Until UPenn's offer arrived, I had never really forced myself to interrogate why I wanted New York so badly. Columbia had the prestige I was looking for, but it also came wrapped in a city I had romanticized for years. I had treated those two things as one package and assumed they were worth paying for.
Penn changed the equation. Philadelphia was not the city I had fantasized about, but UPenn was every bit the kind of elite institution I had wanted. More importantly, the scholarship turned an abstract alternative into a serious option. Suddenly, I could get the academic credibility I was chasing without taking on the same financial burden.
That mattered because I did not come from money. I had saved, but I knew an American master's degree meant much more than tuition: rent, insurance, flights, food, and a year away from full-time work.
For the first time, I had to separate what I actually needed for my career from what I simply wanted because I had fallen in love with the idea of New York.
In the end, I followed my instinct and chose Columbia. The decision was not financially comfortable. Once I sat down and compared the two options properly, I realized New York would require me to borrow money, while Penn's scholarship would let me begin the degree from a much stronger financial position. I knew I was choosing the more expensive path.
What tipped the balance was that I did not see New York as just the place where Columbia happened to be. I saw the city itself as part of what I was paying for. I wanted to study law there, but I also wanted to understand its legal market, meet people working in it, and experience the professional world I hoped to enter.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
In many ways, that instinct proved right. I built relationships there, eventually worked in New York, and had experiences outside the classroom that became central to what came next.
But New York also taught me something I had not expected: the life I had idealized was not necessarily the life I wanted forever. I loved the city's ambition and energy, but eventually realized its relentless hustle culture was not for me.
I later moved to London, changed jobs, and built a career that looks very different from what I imagined when I was choosing between UPenn and Columbia. So, was it all worth it?
I know Columbia gave me a lot. Studying law in the US exposed me to a more practical and participatory style of legal education and helped prepare me for an international career. I am grateful for that experience, and I do not regret having gone.
But COVID made the financial side of my decision harder to ignore. I had chosen the more expensive option partly because I wanted everything that came with being at Columbia and in New York. Then the pandemic changed university life almost overnight. Had I chosen UPenn, I would still have lived through those same disruptions, but I would have done so with a scholarship and more money left in my pocket. Looking back, that difference feels much more significant than it did when I was making the decision.
Years later, I am still paying off two loans from my studies. Columbia helped shape the life and career I have now, and I am grateful I chose it. But every loan payment is also a reminder that UPenn offered me another route, one I will never know the outcome of.

58,2026-09-29,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Brands are increasingly turning to wild, woodsy flavors, from pine sodas to juniper coffee. 
Copied to clipboard
Food’s new favorite flavor is the forest. 
According to a recent report from the New York Times, the next big flavor isn’t pumpkin spice — it’s pine. Food and beverage makers are embracing the taste of forests and meadows. They are betting that consumers will seek those flavors to feel calmer and more connected to nature. 
For example, the cafe K Brew in Knoxville, Tennessee, offers a Smoky Mountain Latte that infuses milk with sage and juniper. Meanwhile, Nashville, Tennessee-based Forest Water offers botanical sodas inspired by nature, including the “bitter, sweet and woodsy” Alpine flavor. 
Additionally, diners in Manhattan have the option of spending $28 on a Forest Old Fashioned at the restaurant 425 Park, made with pine liqueur, spruce tips and alpine bitters. In Pennsylvania, Fox Meadows Creamery offers a popular seasonal winter flavor called Foraged Pine and Rosemary, complete with flavors infused from handpicked white pine needles. 
As another indicator of an industry-wide trend towards woodsy tastes, syrup-maker Torani recently named Forest Pine its flavor of the year. 
“Across markets, we’re seeing consumers show more interest in outdoor-inspired, botanical and earthy flavors,” Ashley Doolin, a product development scientist at PepsiCo, told the Times. 
At chain restaurants like Sweetgreen, the word “foraged” has begun making an appearance. The addition brings the language of a woodland walk to fast-casual dining. Major food companies are taking note; product-development teams are exploring ingredients and flavor cues that promise a closer connection to the natural world. 
“Now we’re starting to see increased interest in flavors that might be about the place, the origin, the landscape or even craftsmanship,” Sally Lyons Wyatt, head of consumer goods and food service at market research firm Circana, told the Times. “Consumers increasingly want flavors that evoke nature, foraging and the outdoors.”
Food trends rarely follow a straight line, per the Times. A flavor can feel inevitable one year and utterly out of step the next, depending on the broader mood. 
Starbucks learned that in 2018, when it introduced a juniper latte. The drink failed to take off, and Starbucks discontinued it the following year. 
The national mood has since grown more uneasy. That anxiety helps explain why nature has become such a potent food cue, said Eve Turow-Paul, a food-trend researcher. 
“Part of this is about control and safety and the loss of control over food sources,” Turow-Paul told the Times. “People don’t trust corporations, and they don’t trust the government. They’re saying, ‘I need to return the locus of control to myself. Lavender and sage and pine and juniper just seem natural. Nature seems safer. It seems less likely I am being duped.’”
Stephanie Mattucci, the principal food and drink strategist for the global food research firm Mintel, told the Times that forest flavors are a way to “ground ourselves.”
“People are attracted to alpine flavors as a way to step away from overstimulation and into the therapeutic benefit of the forest and the trees and mountains,” she said.

59,2026-09-29,"I first talked about how much iPhone video stabilization impressed me when I tested it on the iPhone 13 Pro back in 2021. It’s been improved significantly since then, with both the iPhone 17 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro having a version Apple refers to as “ultra-stabilized video.”
While it doesn’t sound as sexy as some features, it makes a massive difference to the usability of the iPhone for handheld shooting – and my latest test really drove home just how good it is today … 
I have a mini tripod and GorillaPod that I carry with me when I know I’m going to want to shoot some iPhone video footage, but of course, one of the huge benefits of an iPhone is that it’s the camera you always have with you. There are times when I spot an opportunity for a shot and don’t have a tripod with me. 
It’s often possible to improvise, whether that’s by leaning the iPhone against a railing or bracing myself against something like a lamppost. But there are times when I rely on the built-in video stabilization on iPhones, and in the most recent example, shooting with the iPhone 18 Pro, I was incredibly impressed. 
A riverboat trip down the Thames demonstrated that there are times when the iPhone’s built-in video stabilization feature is actually better than a tripod. That’s because the boat rocks around a fair bit, which would have meant a stable shot of the boat with the scenery outside moving around. The iPhone reliably stabilized to the outside world with the boat moving around in the foreground.
That ability to keep the horizon true no matter what movements the platform makes is something we don’t even get with a typical gimbal. Most consumer-grade ones smooth out movement rather than eliminating it. It’s only with high-end professional gimbals and the type of giro mount designed for marine and helicopter use that we get truly locked horizons.
The iPhone 18 Pro’s ultra-stabilization feature honestly delivers this level of performance, and that’s a pretty incredible feature to have as standard in the stock Camera app with no external hardware required.
Check out the sample footage below. If you don’t want to take the full ride, the Tower Bridge section starting here is probably the best showcase for the feature. But if you do want to enjoy the London tourist experience, hit C to switch on the captions for all the bridge names and a few other comments, including pointing out Apple’s UK campus.

60,2026-09-29,"Protests have taken place in Madrid, Barcelona, Seville, Malaga and Cadiz in response to the housing crisis
Spain's coalition government has approved new housing measures following protests over the country's housing crisis, sparked by the eviction of an 87-year-old woman from her home in Madrid last week.
Ministers met on Tuesday to discuss the proposals, which include a ban on evictions until 2030.
Announcing the measures, Health Minister MÃ³nica GarcÃ­a said they would bring relief to more than five million people - but opposition politician MÃ­riam Nogueras said the agreement left ""small landlords out in the cold"".
The proposals - which have been presented in the form of two decrees - come into effect immediately, but must be approved by Congress within a month, although the government hopes to hold the vote as soon as this Friday. 
Pedro SÃ¡nchez's leftist coalition government lacks a majority in parliament and has struggled to get right-leaning parties to approve previous decrees.
In April, a decree seeking to roll over rental contracts was blocked by right-leaning parties.
GarcÃ­a, of the Sumar alliance, said other measures agreed upon on Tuesday included the rolling over of contracts until 2028; stricter regulation of short-term and room rentals; and banning the purchase of housing by investment firms.
""It is time to intervene in the speculative housing market and start striking a balance between ordinary citizens and vulture funds,"" GarcÃ­a said.
Referencing the protests, she added: ""Thanks to the mobilisations and the encampments. Without you, this would not have been possible.""
But Nogueras, spokeswoman for the centre-right Catalan nationalists of Junts party, said: ""We all agreed that evictions - where the property is owned by a fund, a vulture fund, run by someone managing millions of euros - obviously need to be stopped.
""Lumping together in this category those who have invested their savings in a flat, a restaurant or a property - putting them all in the same basket - is a mistake,"" she told Spanish public broadcaster RTVE.
The minority government, which is made up of the SÃ¡nchez's ruling Socialists and junior partner the Sumar alliance, has 30 days from the decrees' publication to reach an agreement in parliament. 
Housing Minister Isabel RodrÃ­guez urged political parties to support the decrees after the eviction of 87-year-old Maricarmen Abascal last week.
""Congress has the opportunity to tell Maricarmen that her suffering was worth it... and that many people will benefit from her battle"", she said.
But Valeria Racu, a spokeswoman for the Madrid Tenants' Union, said the battle had ""only just begun"", and called for more protests across Spain next weekend.
""We all have our eyes on the text, on the small print, to make sure this is the Maricarmen decree,"" added Alicia del RÃ­o, another spokeswoman for the organisation.
Maricarmen Abascal was evicted from her apartment in Madrid on a stretcher on Wednesday
The two decrees approved by the cabinet contain enough to at least suggest that the government has been listening carefully to the demands of the activists who have been protesting in Madrid, Barcelona and elsewhere in recent days. 
The temporary ban on evictions, the rolling over of rental contracts and the ban on investment firms - or ""vulture funds"" as the government has termed them - buying property are all the kinds of policies that have been discussed by housing activists in recent days.
Pablo Bustinduy, minister of social rights, described the measures approved by the cabinet as ""a first step towards the economic and social transformation of the country"".
Bustinduy and his Sumar alliance had entered the negotiations with SÃ¡nchez's Socialists determined to take a stand on this issue, which is one they feel strongly about. 
Sumar's tough stance was a major reason why talks between the coalition partners on the decrees went down to the wire ahead of Tuesday's cabinet meeting.
While the relatively bold new measures may please many on the left, they have raised the hackles of the Junts, whose support is again likely to be needed to approve them. 
Junts and other parties on the right tend to be reluctant to intervene in the housing market, particularly if it means tipping the balance of the law in favour of tenants and against landlords, many of who are their natural electorate.
The question now is whether the social momentum generated by the Maricarmen Abascal case will override such reservations and manage to ensure this package of measures becomes law.

61,2026-09-29,"Real estate photography depends on how many listings agents actually land, so a headline claiming most agents closed nothing in a year hits hard if houses pay your bills. When the numbers behind your client base shift, the way you choose clients, package services, and plan the next few years should shift with them.
A figure circulating on social media said 71% of realtors didn't close a single deal in 2025. Nathan Cool traces that claim to a Redfin presentation, where it was pulled out of context, and points out that it never came from the National Association of Realtors at all. NAR's own data looks very different: 6% of individual agents and 2% of teams reported zero transactions, and the typical member handled about nine deals during the year. Cool has photographed real estate full-time for about 15 years, and he reads these numbers with an eye on who is actually booking shoots. Getting the source right keeps you from cutting prices, pausing your marketing, or leaving the genre over a number that falls apart once you check where it came from.
Experience turns out to be the more useful statistic. The typical NAR member has about 13 years in the business, and income follows tenure closely. Agents with 16 or more years earned a median of $88,000, while those with less than two years made about $8,000, even though a third of all members clear $100,000 a year. Existing-home sales have hovered around 4 million a year for three straight years, the slowest pace since 1995, but the past year didn't bring a sharp new drop. Listings are concentrating among seasoned agents. A brand-new agent might book you once and disappear, while someone with a decade of listings behind them can keep your calendar full for years, which changes who belongs at the top of your outreach list.
Pressure on agents to prove their value has also grown since August 17, 2024, when practice changes from NAR's commission settlement took effect. Buyer agents now sign written agreements before touring homes with clients, and offers of buyer-agent compensation no longer appear on MLS listings, so commissions get negotiated more openly than they did a few years ago. Sellers interviewing listing agents are comparing what each one brings, and the marketing package is one of the few things a seller can see before signing anything. That pulls photos, video, and floor plans closer to the center of an agent's pitch. When you approach a top producer, show your work in terms of their listing presentation: a room before and after editing, the reel that went out the day a listing went live, the floor plan buyers kept asking about. Experienced agents think about time saved and listings won, and a portfolio built around those outcomes gets a different kind of attention than a gallery of pretty kitchens.
Cool builds three recommendations from these numbers, and the first is to stand out. Experienced agents know what good media looks like, so looking like every other shooter in town gives them no reason to switch. He admits that raising your level is harder work, and he connects it directly to landing higher-paying jobs later. His second recommendation is to expand what you offer, from virtual staging and custom edits to reels, walkthrough video, floor plans, and drone coverage. He also takes on the common prediction that agents will simply shoot listings on their phones and fix them with AI, arguing that seasoned agents would rather hand those tasks to a vendor and spend their hours selling homes. His third point, about spreading your work across markets, comes with a clarification he's careful to make. Around 10 years ago, he started adding architectural and design work for remodelers, builders, and designers, later moved into commercial real estate listings, and still counts residential listings as roughly half of what he shoots.
Cool's full breakdown of all three strategies, including where diversification can take you, is waiting in the video above.
Alex Cooke is a Cleveland-based photographer and meteorologist. He teaches music and enjoys time with horses and his rescue dogs."
62,2026-09-29,"Sam Altman has spoken repeatedly in recent weeks about the risks posed by AI agents.
OpenAI on Tuesday unveiled ""dots,"" a new artificial intelligence (AI) assistant they say can proactively carry out tasks on a user's behalf.
At its annual developer day in San Francisco, chief executive Sam Altman referred to ""dots"" as ""remarkably capable, always-on agents that can handle really anything you can think of.""
At the same time, OpenAI president Greg Brockman and other tech leaders held a meeting with President Donald Trump as the firm faces intense scrutiny after internal tests of its AI agents revealed unexpected and occasionally harmful actions.
Dots, however, were pitched as brightly coloured cute cartoons meant to function as digital assistants for people with busy lives.
The dots, Altman said, ""can handle really anything you can think of.""
A glossy video was shown during the event, with people speaking to and naming animated versions of their ""dots,"" then giving them various tasks, like building a website and booking afterschool activities for their children.
Speaking about ""my dot,"" Altman almost entirely eschewed referring to the tool as an agent, which has been in common use for years. AI agents are essentially AI chatbots that are programmed to operate somewhat autonomously.
""You just give your dot a responsibility... and your dots will just get to work and keep working,"" Altman added.    
OpenAI's event on Tuesday took place as President Donald Trump held a White House lunch with tech leaders and House Speaker Mike Johnson.
Trump repeated his belief that AI companies should regulate themselves.
He also said they had discussed creating a ten person committee to oversee the sector, adding there would be ""tremendous self-policing""
Some researchers and company heads have called for a slowdown in AI development as more instances of breaches by AI firms' models become public.
""We need to navigate the centrist path,"" Altman told the BBC during a question and answer session with reporters on Tuesday.
""I am hopeful that the industry will figure out a more sensible, more pragmatic stance than has been exhibited by some of the members of our industry,"" he added, declining to specify particular company names or leaders.
The day prior to OpenAI's event, the company said it was delaying the launch of its latest model due to certain safety issues that showed up during internal testing.
Three takeaways from Trump's 'Super Intelligence' summit
OpenAI scraps rollout of new model over safety concerns
Since July, the company has been dealing with a series of issues with its AI agents acting improperly. It has faced unprompted hacking into the AI platform Hugging Face, posting to third-party websites images that AI agents took from ChatGPT user chats, and accessing non-public information maintained by the Australian government.
OpenAI, as well as rival AI firm Anthropic, previously delayed public release of certain AI models due to potential risks, mostly pertaining to cybersecurity or hacking concerns.
On Tuesday, Altman raised the possibility of ""a legitimate loss of control"" to an AI system, adding ""and you can imagine where we have too much concentration of power in a small number of companies or one company or person or country or whatever"".
Both OpenAI and Anthropic are expect to be listed on the public stock market in the coming months. Anthropic likely will do so this year, while OpenAI is poised for 2027.
Altman said he was eager for his company to sell shares in public markets.
""I think it's bad for the world if OpenAI waits too long to go public,"" Altman told reporters. ""I want people to be able to part in the upside of AI.""
Both companies have lately been clear that they believe AI poses a genuine risk  on a large scale, be it to public infrastructure or public safety.
Speaking last week to the UN, Altman pleaded for ""national and international"" AI standards on measuring the capabilities of an AI tool, assessing related risks, AI safeguards, and the degree to which human oversight over such tools is maintained.
Dario Amodei, the head of Anthropic, told the UN that if the speeding development of AI was not properly managed and overseen, future versions of the technology ""could be a risk to humanity as a whole.""

65,2026-09-29,"Man Faces Up to 10 Years In Prison After Sharing AI Image of a Crocodile
Search
Police in Singapore have arrested and charged a man after he allegedly posted an AI-generated image of a saltwater crocodile swimming in a public water area. 
The accused, 30-year-old Ye Lin from Myanmar, allegedly posted the AI-generated image last month, immediately raising public concern. The image showed an estuarine crocodile in Pandan Reservoir, Singapore’s largest reservoir and a popular recreational area. 
There were real-world consequences. Singapore’s national water agency suspended work at the reservoir for two days to investigate the possible sighting. Reservoir staff and other officials conducted an exhaustive search of the reservoir, ultimately found nothing, and determined the image was fake. Singaporean rowing and canoeing groups had to suspend their scheduled activities during this time. 
Per BBC, Lin intends to plead guilty to the offenses. The charges include distributing a false message. It’s a serious offense punishable by up to three years in prison and a fine of nearly $8,000. 
Lin also deleted the AI-generated image after posting it and the app he used, which disrupted the ensuing investigation into the matter and led to an obstruction of justice charge. This is an even more serious crime and carries a sentence of up to seven years in prison. 
Lin is expected to be in court in November to officially answer for his crimes. The Straits Times says Lin is not expected to employ a lawyer.
“Preliminary investigations revealed that the man had created an AI-generated image depicting a crocodile at Pandan Reservoir and sent it to his colleague. The man subsequently deleted the image and the related application in an attempt to conceal his actions,” the Singapore Police Force said in a statement. 
“The Police will not hesitate to take action against anyone who communicates falsehoods. Apart from causing fear and inconvenience caused to members of the public, such falsehoods also result in public resources being unnecessarily deployed to deal with these incidents,” the police force continued. 
“The Police urge the public to act responsibly and refrain from circulating false information or edited images that could cause unnecessary public alarm. The public should also not submit fabricated or manipulated information, including AI-generated or digitally altered images, to the Police or other Government agencies while presenting such information as authentic.” 
AI-generated images clearly have real-world consequences. In this case, panic and wasted resources could have been better spent elsewhere. In other cases, AI-generated images have even caused global stock market changes. AI-generated images have been used to ruin lives, blackmail people, and are a significant danger to society. While an AI image of a crocodile doesn’t seem like a big deal, it absolutely can be, and may very well land a man in prison.

We’re not going to stand here and tell you, or even imply, that the conservative-dominated U.S. Supreme Court is anything but a font of corruption, given the way it exists primarily to enable 90% of the Donald Trump agenda, objecting only in the rarest of circumstances to defuse bombs like the POTUS’ mail voting executive order that would have functionally destroyed the entire, upcoming midterm election. They really don’t deserve much credit for being rarely forced into action to prevent something Trump is trying to do that would plunge the entire country into a constitutional crisis and blood in the streets. That said, it is nice to at least keep hope alive that in certain circumstances, the justices actually can be shamed and/or bullied into recognizing their duty to say, recuse themselves from a case when they have a vested financial interest in that case’s outcome. 
Cue, Justice Samuel Alito, who this week surprisingly recused himself from a major climate change case after admitting that yeah, the fact that he owns a bunch of stock holdings in energy companies poised to benefit from that case makes him distinctly unfit to rule on it. Imagine that! I know it’s the bare minimum we should be able to expect from a Supreme Court justice, but still: It’s the kind of thing that is nice to see in an administration that is so brazenly corrupt all the time that it becomes surprising any time someone actually admits a conflict of interest does indeed exist. Can you imagine Trump even admitting such a thing is possible for himself at this point? 
Of course, we are cynical enough to immediately come to another assumption here as well: Alito most likely thinks, or knows, that his vote won’t be needed for the desired, energy industry-friendly result, given the conservative dominance the court already possesses. The application of Occam’s razor here would suggest that Alito merely spied a good opportunity for him to look objective in a case, and decided he might as well take it. Still, it feels significant that “Justice Alito has determined that he will not continue to participate in this case,” specifically because his office was arguing back in May that there was no reason for Alito to recuse himself from this exact same case, and that no conflict of interest existed. Or as a court spokesperson said then: “Justice Alito does not have a financial interest in any party. His recusal is not required.” Huh, weird how that changed in the span of a few more months, right?   
“The case could determine whether oil companies face billions of dollars in potential liability for damages from climate change. It is the first matter scheduled to be argued before the court when the justices return to the bench on Monday following their annual summer break.”
By Abbie VanSickle
[image or embed]
— Dean Napolitano (@dinonapolitano.bsky.social) 7:17 PM · Sep 28, 2026

The case in question pits the city and county of Boulder, Colorado against ExxonMobil and Suncor Energy, with the state entities attempting to sue the oil companies for knowingly contributing to the effects of climate change in the state, which they allege among other things has contributed to an increase in destructive wildfires. The energy companies, on the other hand, are naturally arguing that state tort laws are powerless against their monolithic might and that the separation of powers means they’re protected by the cushy federal treatment of the Trump administration and its “let’s pump more mercury into the air!” era EPA.
Alito, meanwhile, doesn’t directly own stock in ExxonMobile or Suncor Energy, but he is loaded in stock for other oil companies such as ConocoPhillips and Phillips 66 according to his financial disclosures, as well as various other firms in the energy sector. A broad ruling from the Supreme Court in favor of the energy companies could effectively provide precedent to cut down many other state level cases of this nature from moving forward, saving fossil fuel giants billions of dollars in potential damages–something observed by ConocoPhillips itself during a recent call with shareholders, where the Colorado case was discussed. Thus, a ruling in favor of the oil companies would be very much in the financial interest of not only the energy industry, but Samuel Alito. Similar cases involving the state-level or even individual impacts of climate change are slowly advancing all over the globe, including cases like the one of this Austrian man with a rare disease who is personally suing his own nation for failing to protect him from the damaging effects of climate change. 
Samuel Alito, meanwhile, has surely not turned over some new leaf of ethical jurisprudence. We are still talking about the man who authored the Dobbs decision stripping federal access to abortion, and the man who was caught on tape not too long ago saying that the country needed to return to “godliness,” which sounds a little like a social opinion from the bench? The single most likely reason for Alito to have recused himself in this particular case is that he’s already confident his own, personal financial interest will already be looked after by the other conservative justices. Still, I guess it’s better than the alternative, where he blatantly casts a vote to further enrich himself, without even caring about the appearance of a kleptocracy. When in doubt, best we at least keep up appearances, right?

For decades, Hunts Point Produce Market in the South Bronx has fed the city while serving as a symbol of environmental injustice. Many of the fruits and vegetables New Yorkers eat are first stored in refrigerated trailers that spew diesel fumes into the poorest, most unhealthy neighborhood in the five boroughs.
By the end of last year, city officials thought they’d finally found a solution. They announced a plan to replace warehouses built in 1967 with a new “state of the art intermodal food distribution facility” that would improve air quality for nearly 13,000 Bronx residents.
But now, the deal — estimated to be worth $650 million — has fallen apart.
What was supposed to have been a major economic development achievement for Mayor Zohran Mamdani has instead led to frustration from Bronx officials who are scrambling to try to salvage a transformative project.
“I've been trying to sound the alarm and try to find ways to get people to pay attention to what is happening,” said City Councilmember Justin Sanchez, who represents the district. “Because it really feels like it has gone unnoticed and just thrown under the rug.”
Some former administration officials blamed Mamdani for taking nearly seven months to appoint a leader at the Economic Development Corporation, the agency in charge of the plan. Others point out that the powerful cooperative of roughly two dozen families who control produce at Hunts Point are hard-nosed, old-school negotiators.
Funding for the project was supposed to come from an assortment of agencies, including $145 million in federal grants and an unprecedented $225 million loan from the U.S. Department of Transportation. But the unraveling of the deal has put the money in jeopardy. The transportation agency recently removed Hunts Point from its list of funded projects in the pipeline.
A rendering of the Hunts Point Produce Market project released by Mayor Adams on his final days in office.
Gothamist interviewed a dozen officials familiar with the deal and history of Hunts Point. They described a once in a generation opportunity to right an environmental wrong. Three mayoral administrations have sought to tackle the pollution at the market in the South Bronx, where one in five children develop asthma.
The deal quietly unraveled over the summer when the cooperative voted down the plan.
“That's the tragedy here,” said Adolfo Carrión, a former Bronx borough president who served as deputy mayor in the Adams administration. “The food arrives at our tables, in our restaurants, in our supermarkets. It presents well. It plates well. But the journey to get there is what we have to fix.”
City Hall veterans say that years-in-the-making projects can face difficult handoffs during transitions – especially between ideological opponents like Mayor Eric Adams and Mamdani. Priorities, attitudes and relationships change when new players arrive at the negotiating table.
The stars appeared to align in recent years after a group of veteran powerbrokers with deep ties to the cooperative and City Hall came together. They included the late Sid Davidoff, the former aide to Mayor John Lindsay, who worked as the cooperative’s lobbyist. Last year Randy Mastro, a former aide to Mayor Rudy Giuliani, joined the Adams administration as first deputy mayor. The former prosecutor had known some of the co-op members since he’d worked under Mayor Rudy Giuliani. Mastro had also represented them as their lawyer.
Nate Bliss, a former top EDC official, said he believed the deal would have gone through had Davidoff not died in November. “He would have made sure City Hall paid attention and understood what was happening,” he said. He added that “transitions are hard” between mayors.
Asked about Hunts Point, Mastro — who is representing multiple clients suing the city — declined to speak at length. “I don’t know why the ball was dropped,” he said. “It was on track when we left.”
Carrión said the mayor’s delay in appointing an EDC head “probably contributed” to the deal’s collapse. Mamdani, a democratic socialist, has said he wants to steer the agency in a new direction that marries economic development with economic justice.
Mayor Adams spoke at Hunts Point in 2022. He announced a deal to transform the produce market on his final days in office.
Speaking on WNYC’s “The Brian Lehrer Show,” Deputy Mayor Julie Su blamed the prior administration for the collapse in negotiations, saying the plan had been “pushed through at the last minute” and without the “buy-in” of those most affected.
The mayor’s office defended its economic development track record.
“Since Day One, EDC has been operating at top speed to advance the Mamdani administration's vision of a city that remains the center of economic growth in America, where public investment delivers material results, businesses can grow and working people can afford to live with dignity,” said Dora Pekec, a mayoral spokesperson, in a statement.
Set on an industrial area stretching more than 100 acres, Hunts Point Market feels a world away from City Hall.
The terminal is a maze of warehouses and storage bays, with trucks coming and going as they serve the city’s demand for produce.
The market is run by scrappy operators getting by on small margins and relentless salesmanship. When he first began working at the market in 1981 at age 16, Danny Kane would watch in amazement as the dealers haggled and hurled insults over the prices of peppers. “It’s a routine they would do every day,” he said.
The market – which is said to be one of the largest in the world – provides up to 60% of the city’s fresh groceries. The products are staples of the city’s food scene: bananas on street carts, avocados at bodegas, mesclun salads at Michelin-rated restaurants and the California-grown honeydew melons used in the U.S. Open’s Honey Deuce cocktail.
“It helps New York be New York,” said Kane, who now leads the 1,400 union members of Teamsters Local 202 who work at Hunts Point. “That’s why you need the market.”
But for decades the market has been using refrigerated storage trailers that run on diesel fuel because operators have run out of warehouse space. According to the city, the site uses up to 1,000 trailers.
Kane said he supported the city’s project. He lobbied for funding from Washington alongside Sen. Chuck Schumer and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, who represents the district. He recalled first hearing rumblings of doubts among the cooperative members in May. As economic development officials presented renderings of the new facility, he noticed the audience shift uneasily in their seats. In hindsight, he said that while Mamdani officials had been engaged and eager to close the deal, they may have wrongly assumed everyone was on the same page.
Three mayoral administrations have sought to reduce admissions at Hunts Point. Then-Mayor Bloomberg spoke with cooperative co-presidents Stephen Katzman and Matthew D’Arrigo in 2011.
“It got blown up by logistics,” he said, adding that most of the operators are old-school and wary of change. “That’s what they are used to. That’s what they know.”
Matthew D’Arrigo, a third-generation owner in the cooperative, said city officials didn’t seem to appreciate the operational challenges of running a market during yearslong construction.
Any change to how produce is stored at Hunts Point had massive implications for how the cooperative members do business. Countless deals over produce are made at Hunts Point every day, with co-op members fielding calls from sellers around the country and contacting local merchants to gauge demand.
The city’s plan involved shrinking the footprint of the market from four to two refrigerated warehouses. The modernized warehouses would eliminate the need for the diesel-powered trailers. But it would require building vertically, a shift that would inconvenience buyers, according to D’Arrigo. Customers at the market want to handle the merchandise.
The co-op’s annual rent would have jumped from $4 million to $14 million, an increase that city officials said would have been used to pay off the federal loan and be offset by the lower operating costs.
“We think long and hard before we start spending money,” D’Arrigo said. “The bottom line was the uncertainties and risks of doing the deal outweighed the risks of not doing it.”
The parties, he added, “kept talking past one another.”
Nico Aguilar, an EDC spokesperson, said the Mamdani administration remained committed to addressing the environmental costs of the market. “Our priority goal is to reduce truck traffic and address the nearly 1,000 diesel-powered trailer units onsite,” he said.
But the mayor’s office hasn’t said whether the city will try to revive the plan. Some observers, including Adams officials, said Mamdani should try to bring the parties back together.
“The relationship between the market and EDC is bigger than any one person,” Bliss said. “The parties got very close once, which means the basic ingredients of a compromise are still there.”
Slumped in a chair inside one of the four warehouses that make up the market, D’Arrigo recounted conversations with mayors dating back to Michael Bloomberg. Behind him hung a 1960s subway advertisement featuring two children holding a bouquet of broccoli.
“This easily was the closest we’ve ever gotten,” he said.
But he and the other merchants have moved on.
“I don’t wake up thinking of the deal,” he said. “I wake up thinking about what the price of strawberries is.”
