There are five undefeated teams left in the NFL, but only three are good enough to be in the top 10. One barely made it into the top 20, showing how much they still need to prove.
So no, winning isn’t everything, especially this early in the season, and especially considering the number of upsets there were in Week 3. 
Here’s a look at where my NFL Power Rankings stand heading into Week 4.
*Super Bowl odds courtesy of DraftKings
Record: 0-3
Previous ranking: 32
Super Bowl odds: +100000Next game: @ Vikings (4:05 p.m. ET)
They hung with the Kansas City Chiefs until the final three minutes on Sunday, and they should hang a banner and print up some hats and T-shirts for that, because that’s probably as good as it’s going to get. They were already in deep trouble offensively, and now they’ve lost RB De’Von Achane for the season with a knee injury. That hurts even more because he would’ve been their biggest trading chip, too. 
Now, a lost season has turned completely hopeless. Seriously, who could the Dolphins possibly beat? Their schedule isn’t terrible, and it’s still nearly impossible to find a win.
Stat To Know: Miami has now started 0-3 in consecutive seasons (2025-2026) for the first time since 1968-1969.
Record: 0-3
Previous ranking: 30 ↓
Super Bowl odds: +50000Next game: @ Ravens (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
At some point, Cam Ward and the offense are going to show some improvement, right? If not, the Robert Saleh era in Nashville will be a short one. Ward was terrible for most of three quarters in a rain-soaked game against the Giants. But even when he got things going, he still ruined it with an awful throw (and decision) for a game-ending interception. They fought the Giants and the Eagles hard, mostly thanks to their defense. 
But Ward still hasn’t looked like the No. 1 overall pick of last year’s draft. In fact, he’s really been the third-best QB of that generally weak class. Good luck turning that impression around with the Ravens and the Texans up next.
Stat To Know: After their loss to the Giants, the Titans have started 0-3 in three consecutive seasons. 
Given Cam Ward's continued struggles, will the Titans be looking to draft a QB early in the draft again next year? (Photo by Johnnie Izquierdo/Getty Images) 
Record: 0-3
Previous ranking: 24 ↓
Super Bowl odds: +10000Next game: vs. Packers (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
They are an absolute mess, and Baker Mayfield is now hurt. But honestly, he wasn’t playing well before his thumb injury. He’s got two TD passes in three games, and he looks unbelievably out of sync with No. 1 target Emeka Egbuka. The numbers say Todd Bowles’ defense is good, but they’re really propped up by back-to-back games against the QB-challenged Browns and Vikings. At this point, he has to be the odds-on favorite to be the first coach fired this season. And maybe soon if he can’t find wins against the Packers, Cowboys or Steelers in the next three weeks.
Stat To Know: After their loss to the Vikings, the Buccaneers are now 2-10 and in their last 12 games dating back to last season. 
Record: 0-3
Previous ranking: 25 ↓
Super Bowl odds: +6500Next game: @ Seahawks (4:25 p.m. ET)
They’re probably too talented to be ranked this low. Then again, this has been the most consistently overrated team in the NFL for about a decade now, so maybe this is just about right. The reality is they’re 0-3 and facing a brutal schedule that could leave them 0-9. They played the Bills close, and that’s nice. But next up is Seattle, Denver, Kansas City, the Rams, Houston, Baltimore. 
Maybe it’s time for Justin Herbert to elevate his team a little? It would also help if his offensive line would protect him and if WR Quentin Johnston would just hold on to the ball.
Stat To Know: The Chargers have now scored fewer than 20 points in six straight games dating back to last season (regular season and playoffs). This is their longest such streak since 1998.
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 28
Super Bowl odds: +20000Next game: @ Bears (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
Geno Smith has been unexpectedly good. He threw for 321 yards and three TDs in a loss to Detroit on Sunday. He also has yet to throw an interception this year. And the Jets’ defense has been good, for the most part. Even giving up 31 points and 381 yards to the powerful Lions is about average. Don’t get me wrong, the Jets still won’t win many games, but they’re a heck of a lot more watchable. 
If the Jets can get through a trip to Chicago on Sunday, they’ve got a very winnable stretch — vs. Cleveland, at New England, vs. Miami, vs. Las Vegas — coming up.
Stat To Know: Kenyon Sadiq's 105 receiving yards is the most in a game by a Jets rookie tight end since Dustin Keller had 107 yards on Nov 9, 2008.
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 27
Super Bowl odds: +30000Next game: @ Giants (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
They really do have some impressive, young, offensive weapons. Head coach Mike LaFleur probably can do a lot this season with RB Jeremiyah Love, WR Michael Wilson and TE Trey McBride. Maybe he’ll eventually get something out of WR Marvin Harrison Jr., too. And QB Jacoby Brissett sure will throw a lot of passes for a lot of yards. But that might just mean they’re going to lose a lot of high-scoring games. 
Arizona will have a shot to beat the broken-down Giants this week. But this team lives in the NFC West, so it's going to take a lot of lumps.
Stat To Know: Jacoby Brissett’s 38 completions against the 49ers marked the third-most in a single game in Cardinals franchise history.
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 20 ↓
Super Bowl odds: +5500Next game: @ Buccaneers (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
This is for all the Packers fans who screamed at me for having them too low when I ranked them … 16th. Was the embarrassing loss at home to the Falcons on Thursday night enough to convince you I’m right about this team? They are a fluky, overtime win against the Jets away from being ranked in the 30s. Turns out, they can’t run at all without the suspended Josh Jacobs. Given the passing trio of QB Jordan Love-WR Matthew Golden-WR Christian Watson, they should be way better than this. 
The Packers better prove it in Tampa this week, though. Otherwise, look out below!
Stat To Know: The Packers allowed 35 points on Thursday, their most in a home opener since 1971. This is also the first time since 2012 that the Packers have lost their home opener, ending a 13 year streak that was tied for the longest such winning streak in NFL history.
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 31 ↑
Super Bowl odds: +12000Next game: @ Saints (Monday, 8:15 p.m. ET)
What in the name of Bijan Robinson was that last Thursday night? This team was a contender for the 32nd spot in this ranking — maybe even the favorite. Then Robinson and WR Drake London just went off versus the Packers. Could it be that all they were missing was the competent play of QB Michael Penix Jr.? 
Maybe head coach Kevin Stefanski can turn this sinking ship around now that he has the right guy at the helm. I need to see it again, though. And I’m not sure whether that will happen because the road ahead is rough, with the Saints, Ravens, Bears and 49ers coming up in October.
Stat To Know: This was the third game in which Bijan Robinson had 150+ rushing yards and Drake London had 150+ receiving yards, the most by any pair of teammates in NFL history.
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 29 ↑
Super Bowl odds: +8000Next game: vs. Colts (Sunday, 9:30 a.m. ET in London)
Marcus Mariota pulled off a big and impressive upset against the defending champs (albeit with the help of some errant throws by Seattle QB Sam Darnold). And that was huge for this battered team, knowing that starting QB Jayden Daniels’ return may only be a couple of weeks away. The Commanders didn’t get much of a running game — and now RB Rachaad White is hurt — and Dan Quinn’s defense offered little resistance until two late interceptions. So there are still issues to be fixed. 
Mariota’s mission is to keep them afloat until Daniels is back. The Colts and Giants are up next, so he’s got a shot.
Stat To Know: The Commanders snapped a six-game home losing streak in their win over the Seahawks. 
Record: 2-1
Previous ranking: 26 ↑
Super Bowl odds: +20000Next game: vs. Steelers (Thursday night, 8:15 p.m. ET)
Two in a row for the Browns? They are so giddy in Cleveland that their fans are suddenly even cheering for Deshaun Watson. He’s still not doing a lot with his array of young weapons (such as WRs Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion and TE Harold Fannin), and their success has been more about their Jared Verse-led defense. 
But hey, they’re out of the cellar, so deal with it. It’ll make for a fun rivalry game against the Steelers on Thursday night.
Stat To Know: With their win over the Panthers, the Browns are now 4-1 in their last five games dating back to last season.
Record: 0-3
Previous ranking: 18 ↓
Super Bowl odds: +3500Next game: vs. Cowboys (Sunday, 1 p.m. ET)
Their defense is too good for this team to be 0-3. But their offense is comically bad, which is why they keep falling. C.J. Stroud hasn’t been a force since he was a rookie, and he has definitely been bad enough to drag them down. Their running duo of David Montgomery and Woody Marks is as bad as it gets, too, probably thanks to a shaky offensive line. 
Here’s the good news: The Texans were 0-3 last year and still made the playoffs and even won a playoff game, and they’re also about to enter the softest part of an already soft schedule. But here’s the bad news: It’s not last year anymore.
Stat To Know: The Texans have fallen to 0-3 in back-to-back seasons. The only teams to make the playoffs after an 0-3 start since 2000 are the 2025 Texans and the 2018 Texans. Can they do it again?
Record: 2-1
Previous ranking: 19 ↓

They beat the Titans in an ugly, wet game, and they’re 2-1, so I get that their fans will be outraged (what else is new?) that they both dropped and are ranked this low. Maybe that’s unfair, and maybe they’ll be OK with games against the Cardinals and Commanders coming up. They certainly could emerge 4-1. But Jameis Winston’s play on Sunday exposed a quarterback problem that won’t go away anytime soon with Jaxson Dart out for the season. And now their best defender, edge rusher Brian Burns, is out for the season. 
It’ll take some wizardry from John Harbaugh to keep this team afloat. It's not impossible, but it won't be easy either.
Stat To Know: Sunday marked the Giants' first win in their last seven games without Jaxson Dart at quarterback.

Yes, this is ridiculously low for a 3-0 team. I get it. But they had a very soft opening, and their schedule is about to get rock hard over the next eight weeks. Besides, do you really believe Kirk Cousins can keep this up? Nine TDs and three interceptions hasn’t been a standard ratio for him in a while. And the Raiders still haven’t really gotten RB Ashton Jeanty going. 
Las Vegas' bigger issue, though? They’ll get the Chiefs, Bills, Rams, 49ers, Seahawks and Broncos over the next eight games. So enjoy this record now, while you still can. It’s hard to see the Raiders pushing much higher.
Stat To Know: Before Sunday, the last time the Raiders scored 30+ points in a regular-season game was on Dec. 14, 2023. It snapped a 39-game streak without reaching 30 points.

Previous ranking: 23 ↑

They showed the Houston Texans what a good defense really looks like — which is incredible considering how bad the Colts defense was the first two games. I mean, they went from giving up more than 500 yards per game to giving up only 223 in Week 3. The Texans' offense isn’t good, but still, that’s an impressive turnaround. If the Colts can get defensive efforts like that, they have more than enough on offense with QB Daniel Jones and RB Jonathan Taylor to make a run at the playoffs. 
Good news for the Colts: They won’t face a really good offensive team again until they travel to Jacksonville in November.
Stat To Know: The Colts snapped a five-game losing streak vs. the Texans on Sunday.

If Jerry Jones’ old man money is really going to be ""dangerous,"" he better do something with it soon, because his expensive defense is blowing yet another season. Two sacks, no interceptions … and has anyone seen Jones’ prize DTs Quinnen Williams and Kenny Clark? Dak Prescott and CeeDee Lamb can’t do it all for this team. And all it would’ve taken was just a little defense to prevent the Cowboys' heartbreaking, last-second loss to the Ravens in Rio on Sunday. 
The Cowboys have the Texans, Bucs and Packers next — three teams with offensive issues. It’s really now or never for the Dallas D.
Stat To Know: CeeDee Lamb notched his 29th career 100-yard receiving game, ranking second in Cowboys franchise history behind only Hall of Famer Michael Irvin.

Remember when everyone thought Mike Vrabel and Drake Maye were going to start another New England dynasty? Yeah, those were fun times … eight months ago. Since then, Maye has gone from near-MVP to turnover machine. And still, it’s hard to say who’s dragging the defending AFC champs down more: him or head coach Mike Vrabel. They can’t run, their defense was a sieve this past weekend against Jacksonville, Maye is a mess … and the defending AFC champs could be finished if they lose in Buffalo this weekend.
Stat To Know: Drake Maye is the first quarterback since 2007 with only one passing touchdown and at least six interceptions through his first three games of a season.

Losing in Cleveland isn’t the embarrassment it used to be, but it still hurts that the Panthers only got in the end zone once. And that’s why the Panthers are languishing in the middle of these rankings. Bryce Young leads the NFL in passing yards, and with weapons like RB Chuba Hubbard and WRs Jalen Coker and Tetairoa McMillan, they are capable of fireworks. But they couldn’t get McMillan going on Sunday and couldn’t find another way to win. 
Expect more offensive fireworks when they face the Lions on Sunday night. Carolina just needs to figure out how to stop Detroit, too.
Stat To Know: Bryce Young has reached 939 passing yards through three games, the second-most passing yards through the first 3 games of a season in Panthers history. 
Record: 1-2
Previous ranking: 15
Super Bowl odds: +8000Next game: vs. Falcons (Monday, 8:15 p.m. ET)
They are so close to being a 3-0 team, and yet they are 1-2 and really so far away. Their latest … um … fumble came against the Raiders when their last four possessions ended with three fumbles and an interception. That spoiled what was previously a brilliant game by QB Tyler Shough. 
With Shough, WR Chris Olave, and head coach Kellen Moore's offensive mind, New Orleans will always score points (though the hamstring injury to RB Travis Etienne hurts). The Saints belong in the top 15. To stay here, they have to stop their stretches of playing stupid.
Stat To Know: Juwan Johnson (2 TDs) and Noah Fant (2 TDs) became only the third pair of tight end teammates in NFL history to each catch 2+ touchdown passes in the same game.
Record: 2-1
Previous ranking: 21 ↑
Super Bowl odds: +5000Next game: @ Browns (Thursday night, 8:15 p.m. ET)
Aaron Rodgers going throw-for-throw with Joe Burrow at his age, and after his ugly start to the season, wasn’t on anybody’s Bingo card. He even dusted off DK Metcalf, his forgotten No. 1 receiver, and RB Jaylen Warren had a big game with Rico Dowdle out. Beating the Bengals was huge, surprising (at least to me), and deserving of a big jump, even if none of this is exactly how head coach Mike McCarthy drew it up. 
It was the first sign of life from the Steelers' stagnant offense. There are no defensive juggernauts in the next five weeks before their bye week, so they’ve got a chance to go on a run if they can keep the scoring up.
Stat To Know: Aaron Rodgers and Mike McCarthy reached 100 regular-season wins together, becoming the seventh starting quarterback–head coach duo to hit that mark. They’re the only duo to do it across two teams.
Record: 2-1
Previous ranking: 14 ↑
Super Bowl odds: +1900Next game: @ Panthers (Sunday, 8:15 p.m. ET)
There aren’t many more dynamic offenses in the NFL, and few teams can boast a dynamic duo like RB Jahmyr Gibbs and WR Amon-Ra St-Brown. This team is a juggernaut, averaging more than 31 points per game. But man, does their defense stink. And it shouldn’t, not with defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (3.5 sacks), yet it still does. 
You know who the Lions remind me of? Last year’s Cowboys. The offense is unstoppable, but it’s the trainwreck defense that’s keeping them out of the top 10. 
Stat To Know: This is the first time in Lions franchise history that they have scored 30+ points in each of their first three games of a season.
Record: 3-0

Oppo Android

Last week, Oppo unveiled the Find X10 for the Chinese market (alongside the Find X10 Pro Max). In the meantime, the global variant of the Find X10 may have run Geekbench. It was spotted in the benchmark's online database with the model number CPH2911.
As you can see in the screenshot below, it managed a single-core score of 2,528 and a multi-core score of 8,709 in Geekbench 7.1.0 for Android. The specific prototype which ran the benchmark had 12GB of RAM. It was of course running Android 17, same as the Chinese version of this phone.
The global Find X10 identifies its chipset as MT6993, which is a model number associated both with the Dimensity 9500 from last year and the Dimensity 9600M that was unveiled earlier this month. The latter is basically just a rebadged Dimensity 9500 anyway, and it's also the SoC powering the Chinese Find X10, so it's pretty safe to assume that the international version of the Find X10 will also have the Dimensity 9600M inside.
Whether the Find X10's other specs will stay the same while it makes the journey from China to other parts of the world remains to be seen. The battery capacity could suffer, at least in Europe, if we look at recent history. But time will tell. In the meantime, you can blindly pre-order a Find X10 in the UK straight from Oppo, and get some exciting freebies and discounts in the process.

91,2026-09-29,"Is a Sony a7S IV Coming? Fat Chance
Search

Ah, rumors, the unsubstantiated gift that keeps on giving. Rumors are often fun to think about, and sometimes they even come true. Color me skeptical that the current hot goss in the Sony world, the a7S IV, will become a reality, though.
As Sony Alpha Rumors writes, “Sony registered the new a7S IV in China!” 
What actually happened is that Sony Japan registered a new, undisclosed camera in China with Wi-Fi 6 support, and Sony Japan typically registers “mid- to high-end models.”  
“My bet is that this will be the new Sony a7S IV,” Sony Alpha Rumors writes. 
“Bet” and “registered the new a7S IV” are very different things. We aren’t convinced it’s a safe bet, either. 
That aside, we’ve discussed a hypothetical Sony a7S IV numerous times over the years, including in 2024. There have been plenty of opportunities to do so, as it’s been nearly six years since the 12-megapixel a7S III hit the market. But just because a camera hasn’t been refreshed in a while doesn’t necessarily mean that a successor is any more likely now. Sony could stop making a7S cameras altogether. The a7S IV has much less of a place in Sony’s ecosystem than the a7S III did in 2020. 
“The important thing to remember is the a7S III — an excellent video camera — came out before the FX3 was released,” says PetaPixel‘s Jordan Drake. “And the FX3, we all know, [is an] incredibly popular, good looking camera.” 
The a7S III and the FX3 share many similarities, including the imaging pipeline, but a wide range of customers rushed to buy FX3 units while the a7S III flew largely under the radar. 
That is not to say that Drake doesn’t like the a7S III (he does) or that he doesn’t want an a7S IV (also yes), but it is hard not to look at the FX3’s success and popularity and think, “Wouldn’t an FX3 II make more sense than an a7S IV?” 
Of course it would. Arguably, given how the FX3 performed in the market, it’s also safe to say that an FX3 II would make the proposition of an a7S IV existing at all even more tenuous. There is a lot of overlap there, and it’s not really Sony’s modus operandi to solve the same problems multiple times in nearly the same way.  
It’s also important to consider that the a7S III was meant to be a very good low-light photo camera. It was really good at that, but with just 12 megapixels, its utility has proven highly limited for stills applications. Its low-light performance isn’t better than what you get when you downscale a higher-res Sony RAW file to 12-megapixel resolution. The a7 V, a7R VI, and a1 II don’t exactly want for high ISO image quality, either, especially when downscaled. 
Today’s online discourse about an a7S IV centers on the new 16-megapixel image sensor in the delayed FX5. It’s a great sensor for video, and it will undoubtedly feature in future video-centric Sony cameras.
“I just don’t think there’s room for a photo-first 16-megapixel, fast-scanning sensor when Sony has an a7 V with a very fast-scanning partially stacked sensor already out there,” Drake says, discounting the sensor’s appeal in an a7S IV-style camera. 
The Sony FX5’s sensor makes more sense in an expected FX3 or FX6 successor than it does in an a7S IV. An FX6 II with the FX5’s 16-megapixel sensor, the same great electronic ND filter, and professional expansion options is an obvious product to launch soon. An FX3 II with the same sensor and the original FX3’s compact form factor makes just as much sense. 
But perhaps we are wrong. It’s happened before and will happen again.
So what if Sony does launch the a7S IV after all these years? After all, the company has shown a willingness to dig a series out of the annals of time before. 
Drake would love to see it. As much as he likes the Sony FX5, he prefers the EVF and overall design of a camera like the a7S series for video work. 
“I would rather have an a7-style body. I could do XLR on the hot shoe. But we keep being told this over and over, videographers hate electronic viewfinders,” he says.  
The a7S III was “always my kind of thing, but clearly the industry disagreed.” 
He can’t see Sony making the a7S IV, and neither can I. Maybe — hopefully — we’re wrong.

Victor 
29 September 2026


Samsung Android Featured Versus

So, you're eyeing a new Samsung foldable but would rather stick with the more traditional and established form factor than go for the new Z Fold8. In that case, the phone you're looking for is now called the Z Fold8 Ultra.
Unlike the Z Fold8, though, which genuinely brings a new shape with few direct alternatives on the market, the same can't quite be said for the Z Fold8 Ultra. You could just as well go for last year's Z Fold7 and get a largely similar experience while saving some money so this leaves us with a dilemma on our hands.
For starters, you can compare the complete specs sheets or directly continue with our editor's assessment  in the following text.
As we mentioned, the Fold form factor is already well established, and Samsung isn't reinventing anything here. The two phones are incredibly similar physically. The Z Fold8 Ultra is technically a hair thinner, but not enough to be noticeable, and the two weigh exactly the same - neither is particularly light.
Samsung hasn't announced any major changes to the hinge mechanism, but it has built on last year's flexible Titanium plate supporting the foldable display by adding another flexible titanium-alloy sheet inside the panel itself. Samsung now calls the whole approach ""Flex Titanium"" technology. The change is meant to make the panel more resilient, particularly to pressure and impact, while somehow also allowing for a thinner overall assembly.
Samsung's press release also claims that the new construction helps ""reduce crease visibility"". That doesn't entirely match our experience. The crease is still very much present on all of the Z Fold8 Ultra units we've seen.
Samsung also says it has fine-tuned the display tension and magnetic force to make unfolding feel ""smoother, lighter and more natural"". It calls this the Easy Open-and-Close Mechanism, apparently borrowing at least part of the concept from the South Korea-exclusive Galaxy Z TriFold. And indeed, you can really feel the difference in the ease of opening.
Separating the two halves of the phone is noticeably easier over the first 5mm or so, where the hinge offers virtually no resistance, and the magnets take less force to overcome. Even so, opening the phone one-handed remains quite difficult and will likely be impractical for most users.
The internal display remains 8 inches in size, just like on the Galaxy Z Fold7, but Samsung has upgraded the flexible 120Hz LTPO OLED panel in several other ways. For starters, the resolution has increased from the Galaxy Z Fold7's 1968 x 2184 pixels to 2256 x 2504 pixels. HDR10+ video support is once again present, though Dolby Vision remains absent.
Samsung also advertises the Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra's display as being brighter than the Galaxy Z Fold7's. Arguably more important, however, are the new Flex Titanium construction and the anti-reflective coating on the internal screen. It does make a noticeable difference to outdoor legibility, though it's not as good as some competitors like the Magic V6.
The cover display, meanwhile, appears unchanged from the Galaxy Z Fold7, and Samsung doesn't advertise any upgrades here either. It's still a 6.5-inch LTPO OLED panel with a 120Hz refresh rate and a resolution of 1080 x 2520 pixels.
Our testing didn't reveal a meaningful increase in maximum brightness with a 75% white test pattern. The Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra reached around 1,400 nits in automatic mode, broadly matching the Galaxy Z Fold7. It did, however, come close to Samsung's advertised 3,000-nit figure in our 10% white-patch test. Combined with the anti-reflective coating, this makes the new internal display noticeably easier to read in bright sunlight despite the similar full-screen brightness.
The cover display produced similarly impressive results, approaching 3,000 nits with a 10% white patch and reaching around 1,400 nits with a 75% white pattern - once again broadly in line with the Galaxy Z Fold7. Excellent performance in either case.
The Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra brings a meaningful battery upgrade over the Galaxy Z Fold7, even if its capacity is still not particularly large by current standards. Other phones making the switch from conventional Li-Po batteries to silicon-carbon cells have delivered more substantial gains, but the increase from 4,400mAh to 5,000mAh is still very welcome.
Our battery tests show a notable improvement not only in the overall Active Use Score, but also in the screen-on tests, including web browsing and video streaming. The bump up in Active Use Score is even more impressive all around when using the cover displays on both phones.
The Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra now supports 45W wired charging over Power Delivery with the appropriate PPS profile, up from 25W on the Z Fold7. Wireless charging has also been bumped from 15W to 20W.
These are far from minor upgrades. It's almost hard to believe that last year's Samsung foldables were still limited to 25W wired charging, but the difference is very real in practice. The Z Fold8 Ultra shaves roughly half an hour off a full charge, which is a substantial improvement considering that this amounts to around a third of the Z Fold7's entire charging cycle. That hardly makes the Z Fold7 unusable, of course, but the Z Fold8 Ultra is noticeably more convenient in this respect.
This is one area where the Z Fold7 surprisingly ekes out a small win over the Z Fold8 Ultra, not because it has a particularly impressive speaker setup, but because Samsung has arguably made the newer model a little worse.
The Z Fold8 Ultra is slightly quieter than its predecessor, just barely missing the cut for a Good rating in our standardized testing. That may not even be its biggest issue, though, as the tuning has changed as well.
Music tracks sound fuller and deeper, but the stronger bass tends to overpower the mids and vocals, making the overall presentation sound somewhat muffled. We prefer the older tuning - the Z Fold7 sounds cleaner and louder.
Since both of these are proper Samsung flagships, they naturally get the full flagship treatment. In both cases, that means whatever top-tier Qualcomm chip was available at the time of release. Samsung also uses the ""for Galaxy"" versions of these chips, which typically run at slightly higher clock speeds than their standard Snapdragon counterparts.
So, the older Z Fold7 gets the Snapdragon 8 Elite, while the Z Fold8 Ultra moves to the newer Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5. The latter is clearly more powerful, though there are some nuances to that, which we'll get to in a bit.
Both are still thoroughly modern 3nm chips packed with up-to-date features and connectivity options. Aside from the upgrade from Bluetooth 5.4 on the Z Fold7 to Bluetooth 6.0 on the Z Fold8 Ultra, there isn't really any meaningful connectivity difference to speak of, which is good news for buyers considering the older model.
Samsung hasn't changed the memory configuration in any meaningful way either. Both phones come with 12GB of RAM as standard and can be had with 16GB in some higher-end variants. Storage starts at 256GB and goes up to 1TB on both, with comparable UFS 4.x-class storage. Neither phone offers expandable storage.
This is also a good place to briefly touch on software. Technically, the Z Fold8 Ultra has the advantage here since it is one of the launch devices for One UI 9, based on Android 17. The Z Fold7 should eventually get One UI 9 as well, along with most, if not all, of its new features. That seems especially likely since One UI 9 doesn't introduce any major feature that appears to depend on new hardware. It's mostly a refinement of the existing experience, with a few new Galaxy AI additions thrown in.
The Z Fold8 Ultra also has a technical advantage when it comes to long-term support. Both phones come with the same excellent seven-year software support promise, but being a year newer, the Z Fold8 Ultra naturally has an extra year of support remaining. In practice, though, relatively few buyers are likely to keep a flagship foldable for six or seven years.
Looking at the benchmark results, it's clear - and entirely expected - that the Z Fold8 Ultra has more burst performance to offer across the board. The difference isn't huge, though, especially in broader benchmark suites such as AnTuTu.
Then there's thermal throttling, and neither phone does particularly well here. The Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra is supposed to use an expanded graphite cooling structure but it still loses around half, and at times even more, of its peak performance under prolonged load. That's hardly unusual for phones powered by modern flagship Qualcomm chips. Even conventional slab phones struggle to cool them effectively, and the foldable form factor only makes the job harder.
In an interesting twist, however, the Z Fold7 actually throttles less aggressively than its successor. So, in something like a prolonged gaming session, the real-world performance gap between the two may end up being fairly small. That's good news for prospective Z Fold7 buyers.
The Z Fold8 Ultra doesn't introduce any major changes to the camera system. It's almost entirely the same as on the Z Fold7, save for the new ultrawide camera, which swaps the previous generation's conventional RGB sensor for a Tetrapixel one while keeping the same optical format.
Either way, it's a triple rear setup headlined by what appears to be the same 200MP main camera. That's joined by an ultrawide on one end - 50MP on the Z Fold8 Ultra and 12MP on the Z Fold7 - and a modest 3x telephoto on the other, producing 12MP photos from a 10MP sensor. Then there are two 10MP selfie cameras, one for each display, with the internal one using a much wider lens (17mm vs. 23mm). Nothing appears to have changed there.
The camera samples, both photo and video, were taken on separate occasions with the two phones.
Daylight
Regardless of which one you get, you can expect excellent daytime shots from the main camera, with great dynamic range and vibrant colors. Samsung does appear to have tweaked something, though, as the newer model has a slight edge in sharpness. It's hardly a dramatic difference, and mostly an academic one at that, so it certainly shouldn't be a deciding factor between the two.










Daylight comparison, main camera (1x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
There's a bit of that at 2x as well, and it's somewhat more easily noticeable here - the Z Fold8 Ultra's shots are sharper than the Fold7's.










Daylight comparison, main camera (2x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
At 3x, we wouldn't say we're seeing any of that difference - the two telephotos capture essentially the same images.










Daylight comparison, telephoto (3x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
On the ultrawide end, we'd struggle to find a meaningful difference either - yes, it's a different sensor, but quality is just about the same.










Daylight comparison, ultrawide camera (0.6x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
Low light
In the dark, main camera photos at 1x are very similar, both models being quite conservative with the low-light processing and serving relatively soft-ish and noisy, but otherwise alright images.










Low-light comparison, main camera (1x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
Both phones produce sharper and cleaner images in their dedicated Night modes, though on the Z Fold8 Ultra, the option needs to be enabled through Camera Assistant. Interestingly, the older model actually does a better job here, with less aggressive sharpening and none of the Z Fold8 Ultra's tendency to distort colors.










Low-light comparison, main camera (1x), Night mode: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
At 2x in full auto mode, the Z Fold8 Ultra's photos are definitely noisier, but they're also sharper more often than not. The newer model maintains that sharpness advantage in Night mode as well, where it also manages to clean up the noise nicely most of the time.










Low-light comparison, main camera (2x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7










Low-light comparison, main camera (2x), Night mode: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
At 3x zoom, neither phone produces particularly impressive photos, though the Z Fold7 is perhaps a touch more consistent in full auto mode, as the Z Fold8 Ultra can get very noisy on occasion. In Night mode, the newer model improves more noticeably, but we'd still call it mostly a tie. We're also not particularly fond of what appear to be AI-generated, mesh-like textures on some building facades.










Low-light comparison, telephoto (3x): Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7










Low-light comparison, telephoto (3x), Night mode: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
The new ultrawide occasionally has a slight sharpness advantage in low light, though not consistently. It does seem to offer better dynamic range, particularly in the highlights. In Night mode, the Z Fold8 Ultra shows some of the same color issues and texture enhancement we've seen elsewhere, along with heavier sharpening. Even so, the newer model's 0.6x results are somewhat better overall.










Low-light comparison, ultrawide camera: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7










Low-light comparison, ultrawide camera, Night mode: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
Selfies
We initially thought Samsung hadn't changed anything about the selfie cameras, but side-by-side comparisons reveal that at least the focus distance on the cover camera has been tweaked. The newer model captures sharper facial detail and produces a touch more background blur. The same appears to be true of the internal camera as well. Overall, it's only a minor difference, but we'll still count it as an improvement.





Selfies comparison, cover cameras: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7





Selfies comparison, internal cameras: Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra • Galaxy Z Fold7
Below, we have a few frame grabs from videos recorded by the two phones at each focal length to make direct comparison easier.
The Z Fold8 Ultra supports the full range of video recording features Samsung currently offers - Pro mode, codecs, resolutions and frame rates are all covered. It does have a couple of advantages over the Z Fold7, namely Samsung's pro-grade APV codec and Horizon Lock stabilization, but that's about it. We imagine those extras will matter to only a fairly small group of users.
Daytime video quality is essentially identical on the two phones across all four preset zoom levels. We're not seeing any meaningful advantage from the Z Fold8 Ultra's new ultrawide camera, and the sharpness edge its main camera showed in stills at 1x and 2x doesn't carry over to video. The 3x clips are virtually identical as well.





Daylight video screengrabs, Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra: 0.6x • 1x • 2x • 3x





Daylight video screengrabs, Galaxy Z Fold7: 0.6x • 1x • 2x • 3x
Low-light video from the Z Fold8 Ultra's ultrawide is better - it's got better highlight preservation and it's also sharper. The main camera also has a slight dynamic range advantage and possibly resolves a bit more detail. 2x clips aren't great, but are generally usable from both, while the 3x cameras are best avoided, especially that on the Z Fold7.





Low-light video screengrabs, Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra: 0.6x • 1x • 2x • 3x





Low-light video screengrabs, Galaxy Z Fold7: 0.6x • 1x • 2x • 3x
The Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra is clearly the better phone of the two, but perhaps not by as much as the name and price premium might suggest. Samsung has improved several important areas, yet the fundamental Fold experience remains remarkably similar to what you already get with the Galaxy Z Fold7.
The Z Fold8 Ultra has the nicer internal display, with a higher resolution, an anti-reflective coating and a somewhat less noticeable crease. Its larger 5,000mAh battery also delivers significantly better endurance, while the jump from 25W to 45W wired charging makes a very tangible difference. You also get more peak performance from the newer Snapdragon, slightly better image quality in a few scenarios, improved selfies, and a newer software baseline with an extra year of support remaining.
Still, the Galaxy Z Fold7 gets surprisingly close in most other respects. It's essentially identical in size and weight, has the same 6.5-inch cover display and 8-inch inner-display form factor, and its cameras produce very similar results most of the time. Its Snapdragon 8 Elite remains extremely fast, while the older phone actually throttles less aggressively under prolonged load. The Z Fold7 also has the better speakers, and its camera processing can occasionally deliver preferable results at night.
That makes the choice largely a matter of price. If money is no object, the Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra is the obvious pick - the improvements to battery life, charging and the internal display in particular make it the more complete device. But if you can get the Galaxy Z Fold7 for substantially less, it remains an excellent alternative and arguably the better value. The Z Fold8 Ultra is a worthwhile refinement of the formula rather than a reinvention of it, and existing Z Fold7 owners in particular should feel little pressure to upgrade.

      Singapore
  



      Singapore
  

""I was very sensitive to anything wrong being done because of the reputation of the individuals in the board and even to my own reputation,"" Dr Goh Jin Hian said as he took the stand.
Goh Jin Hian arrives at the State Courts on Sep 29, 2026. (Photo: CNA/Syamil Sapari)

       This audio is generated by an AI tool.
    
SINGAPORE: Former chief executive officer of investment holding company New Silkroutes Group (NSG) Dr Goh Jin Hian told the court on Tuesday (Sep 29) that he was unfamiliar with the market-making industry when the company engaged designated market maker GTC Group in 2018, but believed its services were ""completely legal"".
The 57-year-old son of former prime minister Goh Chok Tong also described how he was very sensitive to any wrong being done because of the reputations at stake, including his own.
Dr Goh took the stand in his false trading trial for the second day on Tuesday. He is accused of conspiring with other executives from NSG, a publicly traded company, to push up the share price as the company's business strategy was premised on a high share price.
He is contesting charges of conspiring to create a misleading appearance of NSG's share price, and of ""doing things with the purpose of creating a misleading appearance of NSG's share price"".
Dr Goh is on trial alongside NSG's former chief corporate officer, 55-year-old Kelvyn Oo Cheong Kwan. They are accused of engaging in the conspiracy, along with market maker Huang Yiwen and former NSG finance director William Teo Thiam Chuan, both of whom have been convicted and sentenced.
Dr Goh told the court that NSG was under ""some pressure"" to lift a months-long suspension on trading in its shares, which he believed were already thinly traded. Thinly traded securities are financial assets that are difficult to buy or sell without affecting their market price.
When he and NSG met Huang, the sole shareholder and director of GTC Group in February 2018, there was little time to engage a market maker before lifting the trading suspension. Market makers refer to financial firms or individuals who keep markets liquid by offering prices at which they will buy and sell an asset. Under SGX rules, designated market makers are required to provide competitive bid and offer quotations to provide for an adequately liquid market.
He also addressed the S$60,000 (US$46,900) monthly fee GTC charged, which he considered high but agreed to after consulting his team.
""I didn't know the market rate, I didn't know the industry at all,"" he said.
Dr Goh said he first met Huang in February 2018, in what was their only meeting.
Huang had been introduced to NSG through Teo, after Dr Goh asked the latter if he knew any market makers who could ""support shares and prevent Shen killing the price"" when the company's trading suspension was lifted.
This referred to NSG's former director and substantial shareholder Lee Soek Shen, whom Dr Goh believed was selling large volumes of his shares, exerting downward pressure on NSG's share price.
Dr Goh testified that he had learnt it was possible to engage a market maker to provide liquidity for NSG's shares and believed it to be ""completely legal"" to do so. 
During their meeting, Huang explained market making to Dr Goh and described GTC Group as a designated market maker on SGX. 
According to Dr Goh, Huang said his services could mitigate short-term volatility caused by Mr Lee's selling and, over time, allow NSG's shares to trade towards what Dr Goh considered their fair value.
NSG had requested a trading halt in November 2017, followed by a voluntary suspension of its shares in December while it negotiated acquisitions.
After nearly three months of suspension and just days after announcing three major deals, the company said in February 2018 that its shares would resume trading the following day.
Asked whether he or NSG had tried to meet with other market makers, Dr Goh said he did not know where to find them. 
There was also ""some pressure"" to lift the suspension, as they had completed some deals and had ""very little time"" to engage a market maker beforehand.
NSG eventually engaged GTC, which charged S$60,000 a month.
Dr Goh said his first reaction upon seeing that amount listed in the terms of agreement between the two was that it was three times his salary.
""I thought it was high in (that) regard. I didn't know the market rate, I didn't know the industry at all,"" he said.
He consulted his team, including Teo, on whether NSG could afford the fee and was assured that it could.
The term ""whiter than white"" was brought up again in the trial, this time with the defence asking Dr Goh if he recalled the use of this phrase during the February 2018 meeting.
Dr Goh said he remembered the phrase being uttered by Oo to inform Huang that his conduct in providing market-making activities for NSG had to be completely legal and comply with rules by the Monetary Authority of Singapore and SGX.
Dr Goh said he agreed with the sentiment, saying: ""I was very sensitive to anything wrong being done because of the reputation of the individuals on the board and even to my own reputation.""
Without divulging his own background and corporate experience, he testified that most, if not all, the individuals on the board were people of good repute. Oo was a lawyer by training and was ""not going to do anything illegal"", Dr Goh said.
He told the court that the board members and he were mindful of their status and responsibilities to the company.
The defence delivered its opening statement on Monday, arguing that Dr Goh did not know many key details of what Teo arranged or agreed with Huang. 
Even Teo appeared not to know what Huang and GTC Group were doing, said his lawyers - Senior Counsel Tan Chee Meng, Mr Paul Loy, Mr Samuel Navindran and Ms Lee Jiayi from WongPartnership - in the statement.
They also sought to put several WhatsApp messages Dr Goh had sent to his management team in context, describing some of the exchanges as casual conversation ""akin to banter"".
In one, Dr Goh wrote: ""looks like we can decide how much to price our shares today"" and ""maybe open at S$0.30?"", accompanied by a smiling face with sunglasses emoji.
The defence said he was being ""somewhat facetious"", arguing that SGX rules would have made it impossible to move the share price in the manner suggested.
They noted that the seemingly questionable messages amounted to ""loose and casual language"", rather than reflective of any improper intent to manipulate the market, and that there was never any conspiracy between Dr Goh and  Huang.
""For the sake of argument, even taking the worst possible interpretation of Dr Goh's conduct, he would at the most have been reckless as to whether what he was doing might result in a false appearance being created,” said the lawyers.
""It was never his intention, nor his purpose, to do so.""
The trial continues.
Teo was given 12 weeks' jail in September 2024, while Huang, who was involved in conspiracies to rig the market or price for share or unit counters, was sentenced to jail for over two years in August last year.
If convicted, Dr Goh and Oo face a jail term of up to seven years, a fine of up to S$250,000, or both for each charge.

Dear patient readers,
Forgive me for not writing an Iran war post, but there is far too much noise in the signal. Iran foreign minister Araghchi is still in the US, with indirect talks taking place between mediators. The lack of direct meetings is continued proof of lack of trust. The Iran move to embarrass the US into engaging IMHO is a PR show more than a serious measure (although one has to be prepared for out-of-the-box responses). Recall that talks contain oil price increases so Trump has reason to follow Iran’s lead. 
But it is hard to see these conversations as other than for show. Iran knows the US generally and even more so under Trump is not agreement capable. It seems vanishingly unlikely that Trump could bring himself to take the up-front steps needed to counter that, such as releasing all the frozen funds. And the US cannot deliver Israel’s compliance.
And most important, Iran has not reached a key war aim, of inflicting so much pain on the US and Israel that they are deterred from attacking Iran again for at least a generation. 
In keeping, the US continues to move air assets into the region, although Larry Wilkerson on Mario Nawfal said there were none of the usual indicators of imminent attack. 
As for Strait of Hormuz transits, despite knee-jerk rejections by some Iran-sympathetic commentators, it’s clear that oil is getting out, and perhaps even in decent volumes. Yesterday, we highlighted an article by Nicholas Wade, The Vulnerabilities Behind the Gulf Export Recovery. Wade went carefully through what can reasonably be known and found that the transits now are in the 8-12 million barrel a day range. Keep in mind the pre-war level was 15 to 20 million barrels a day. So the world is still starved of crude, just at not as severe a level as say a month ago. 
We pointed out about a week ago that none other than Professor Marandi confirmed that, although he said some of the shipments were of Iranian oil transported through Iraq. 
As you will see below, the apparent movement is or could be so high that one expert claimed that Iran had lost control of the Strait of Hormuz. The counter-argument is that the key to Iran prevailing is not the threat to create a global depression but destruction of US military assets in the region.
There may be an additional explanation, as in dark matter, as in pressure from China. China thought it was so important to blunt the global impact of the choking of the Strait of Hormuz that it stopped buying oil on global markets entirely and drew down its (admittedly vast) strategic petroleum reserve. China has now resumed oil purchases but not at its pre-war level. 
China’s domestic economy is weak. Its biggest export markets are Southeast Asia and Europe. Both are hit hard by the energy crunch. Even though China is not as export dependent as it once was, with internal demand flagging, a global recession or worse depression would hit China hard and intensify what more and more looks like a Japan-style balance sheet recession. 
In other words, this is one of those times when sitting things out for a day or two informationally on the big issues on the Iran war front might allow for some fog to clear.

When they were talking about copper and bronze and iron swords, it puts me in mind of a scene from the 1954 film “The Egyptian”-
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5y1ovnPT6wI (1:55 mins)
The problem was that to make bronze, you needed tin. And this required a trading network that stretched from Cornwall to Afghanistan. If you had a break down of those trading routes then that would mean no tin in meaning no bronze. It was only the existence of those major powers that could guarantee those trade routes so when they went away, so did those trade links.
And now China has the near monopoly on rare earths needed for modern batteries, navigation, and communications.

Key graf that you knew the article was working up to
To understand the political consequences of new technologies, we need to understand how they shape power. Some technologies, like castles, change the offense-defense bias – whether it is easier to attack or defend. Others, like iron, change who has access to weaponry. A clear modern-day parallel is drones, which have been transformative in the Russia–Ukraine war and are reportedly responsible for up to 80 percent of recent casualties in that conflict as of 2026. A drone costing just a few hundred dollars can threaten a tank, artillery gun, or command post worth many times more. Drone combat has lowered war’s cost of entry and could make it easier for small guerrilla groups to challenge dominant powers.
Or how about the way IEDs transformed the Iraq occupation? To quote an ever useful cliche: “necessity is the mother of invention.” Imperial hubris on the other hand is the mother of nemesis.
The “democratization of violence,” as the writer put it, does tend to make large bureaucratic overweening government less sustainable.
“The people of the various provinces are strictly forbidden to have in their possession any swords, short swords, bows, spears, firearms, or other types of arms. The possession of unnecessary implements makes difficult the collection of taxes and dues and tends to foment uprisings.”
–Toyotomi Hideyoshi

In Praise of Obsessive Men. 
It is an intriguing essay by Rosemary Hopcraft.
As I man, I am not sure that I find the behavior she describes obsessive. Feynman was a remarkably productive human being at many levels, and working calculus problems all day may have impaired his relationship with his wife. Yet he was also known for his generosity and sociability. Oppenheimer and learning Nederlands: He made a commitment and he kept it. That’s what a man is supposed to do.
And yes, agreed, a different bell curve for men, with more geniuses and more scoundrels. There is a story that Michelangelo used to work endlessly, to the point that his boots adhered to his lower legs and had to be eased off his body. 
Yet, setting aside the word “obsession,” which may veer toward psychologizing, we also know that men are trained to master subjects and techniques, to learn crafts, to deal with the physical world effectively, and to show skill. What’s surprising is that this behavior is now considered somewhat surprising.
Contrariwise, one of the biggest hollow slogans sold to women is that women are better at “multitasking.” As if there is such a thing as “multitasking,” which is pretty much just fracturing of the attention span. 
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surely_You're_Joking%2C_Mr._Feynman!

Michelangelo and the dog-skin boots stuck to his legs:

Michelangelo, despite being a legendary creative genius, had poor personal hygiene habits. His biographer Ascanio Condivi described how he often slept in his clothes and boots due to cramp, leading to skin issues. Giorgio Vasari remarked that his long-worn dog-skin buskins were so bonded to his legs they became part of him.

RE: “Great Leaps in Biological Theory”
This link describes how, I believe, great advances in Science are accomplished. What might be termed the “hypothesis-experiment” paradign and the enthusiasm for statistics and correlation research — which I believe fits nicely into the supposed advantage of research contracts instead of research grants — nicely destroys much of the creativity that goes into coming up with hypotheses. Too many of the hypotheses tested by contract science have reduced u.s. science to brick laying in a search for new products and instant profit from a suggestive correlation validated using statistics.
This extract from the link captures some of the harm the Neoliberal science has accomplished:
“But the theory wasn’t met with enthusiasm among his contemporaries, and Mitchell, who also suffered from gastric ulcers, left academia in 1963 to set up his own organization, the Glynn Research Institute, with his family’s financial support.5″
5:”What might have become of Mitchell’s work had it remained dependent on the conventional research grant system?”
Feyerabend’s Against Method is over fifty years old now, but he pointed out nicely back then how real science as practiced by some of the more famous exemplars did not conform to the Popperian ideal, especially when it came to major advances. Of course he got roundly lambasted for having the presumption to show that there’s more to science than the prevalent mythology entailed. A good example of postmodern thinking that was not mired in the elite preoccupations of French Theory.

Oil through Hormuz climbs closer to pre-war levels
Oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz averaged about 13.2 million barrels per day over the past week – roughly 77 percent of their pre-war level – even as Iran claims to have stepped up attacks on vessels trying to cross the waterway, according to Kpler.
Daily confirmed oil flows varied sharply from September 24-27, ranging from about 6.5 million to 12.6 million bpd. The figures for the most recent days are preliminary and may rise as more shipments are confirmed.
The increased movement of oil comes during ongoing talks to fully reopen the strait within seven days, a plan proposed by Iran.

Why are we wasting radioactive waste? After watching the “Martian” a few times I started wondering whether there might be some way to package radioactive waste, like the package around the Radioisotope Thermoelectric Generator (RTG) that Mark Watney uses to keep himself warm inside his Mars vehicle. Instead of HEAP, the state of New York could make packaged radioactive heat sources available to warm the homes of the aged and needy. As long as the radioactive source did not produce too many gamma rays, couldn’t shielding stop alpha and beta particle radiation? The aged and indigent would make an excellent population for testing the effectiveness of various shielding packages to enable designing the least costly, but still sufficiently effective shielding package.\s
The problem here is how to you ensure that that radioactive material is safely secured against idiots and thieves. Back in ’87, some scavengers were working through an abandoned hospital in Brazil when they came across a machine for radiation therapy. The results were tragic and widespread-
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goi%C3%A2nia_accident
Ah yes, I’ve heard of that one. Horrible incident.
If this is a serious question, then the serious answer is – it depends on the sort of waste we are talking about. 
The article linked above specifically concerns low level radioactive waste. Much of it isn’t even Uranium or Plutonium, but rather the immediate byproducts of radioactive decay like Caesium. They do continue to decay, but we are not talking anywhere close to the level of heat and ionizing radiation emissions of the high-grade stuff.
The really high-grade stuff, like spent fuel from nuclear power plants, of which the US generates an average of 2k tons a year (not quite evenly distributed, however, due to 18-month refuelling cycles), is the stuff that really generates a lot of heat. I mean, the biggest issue at Fukushima were basically the spent fuel pools drying up, and the ensuing chemical reaction leading to an explosion and dispersal of radioactive material. However, one, putting a bunch of that stuff into the boiler room of a retirement home is…well, nothing is theoretically impossible, but I wouldn’t want to see someone try it. Two, the real solution that scientists have been chasing for many years is to reprocess this spent fuel into more un-spent fuel; the Russians claim to have finally come up with a true 100% closed fuel cycle, but the prototype isn’t supposed to come online until next year. But in any case, that would be a more efficient way to deal with the problem (not counting transportation issues, which are a thing) than irradiating some stray seniors.
I thought ‘\s’ indicated sarcasm. Should I have used ‘/s’ instead?

Prices are at the top of consumers’ minds, with more respondents saying their personal finances were bad as opposed to good.

AI, the moral panic continues
OpenAI Ignored Employees Who Warned It Wasn’t Doing Enough About Security (NY Times; archive)
Months before OpenAI’s artificial intelligence went rogue, two employees raised an alarm with top executives. They were ignored.
In emails, the employees said they worried that OpenAI’s newest artificial intelligence models were not being appropriately monitored during testing to gauge the technology’s sophistication and to secure the models, according to messages viewed by The New York Times.
Also regarding “AI”, albeit slightly tangential to the “AI is going to kill us all” worry, there is the wee matter of productivity and quality. Software developers are among the willing guinea pigs earliest serious adopters, and thus especially interesting to consider for what “AI” can really promise to industry in the future. 
The following report from Faros is worth a look:
Ten takeaways from the AI Engineering Report 2026: The Acceleration Whiplash
https://www.faros.ai/blog/ai-acceleration-whiplash-takeaways
Two years of telemetry. 22,000 developers. More than 4,000 teams. 
The AI Engineering Report 2026 is not a survey of how developers feel about AI. It is a measurement of what AI is actually producing across the full software development lifecycle, tracking metric change between periods of lowest and highest AI adoption within each organization. 
What it found has a name: the Acceleration Whiplash. AI has flooded a system built around human-paced development and human-quality code with output it was never designed to absorb. 
Throughput is up. So are bugs, incidents, and the hidden costs accumulating at every stage downstream. 
Scroll down for the infographic. TL;DR : Output is up, while production quality is declining.
The incidents-to-PR ratio is up 242.7% as teams move from low to high AI adoption. An incident is an outage, security event, or system failure reaching real users in production systems across finance, healthcare, infrastructure, and every other sector where software runs critical operations. For every PR merged, incidents are occurring at more than three times the rate relative to the low AI adoption baseline. This is a ratio, not a probability: a single PR can be linked to multiple incidents, and not every incident traces directly to the most recent merge. The figure establishes that the relationship between merged code and production failures has deteriorated dramatically as AI adoption has scaled. Monthly incidents are up 57.9%. What started as a productivity conversation has become a reliability problem. […]
In our 2025 AI engineering report on the AI Productivity Paradox, bugs per developer were up 9% as AI adoption grew. In this dataset, that figure has risen to 54%. The relationship between AI adoption and defect rate is not flattening as organizations mature their AI programs; it’s steepening. More AI-generated code in the codebase correlates with more bugs per developer, and that relationship is strengthening as adoption deepens.
That’s been my concern;
Imagine that, tools that don’t truly understand the architectural nuance and business goals of your software, introduce bugs.
It’s effective at dumping out “functional” one-shot prototypes through brute force.
I worry about the future as more mission critical software becomes infected with LLM outputs.

re: Li Xunlei on China’s Transport Overcapacity
The article is very loosely argued. To be economical with time, I will focus on the graphs presented.
1) The variable presented on the y-axis (tonnes-kilometer of freight transported, per kilometer of expressway in province) is already normalized by the metric on the x-axis (expressway length), so we wouldn’t expect to see a trend even if the tonnes-kilometer of freight transported were directly proportionate to the length of the road for transporting that freight. The inclusion of literal cities (Shanghai, Beijing, Tianjin, Chongqing) just highlight the material heterogeneity between provincial-level administrative units of China. And those who have studied a little of the geography of China, even a poor student like myself, might notice that the provinces with the lowest freight intensities (near the bottom of the graph), are all very mountainous: Tibet, Qinghai, Guizhou, Yunnan, and Sichuan. The outlier is the island of Hainan, where one suspects that the lion’s share of freight would be transported through the sea lanes.
2) As the author himself remarked, the graph does not include transportation by private cars. Given that the number of private cars in China has increased by something like 10-fold over the period depicted, it’s hard to argue that the decrease in passenger transport intensity by public motor vehicles is evidence for a decrease in overall passenger transport intensity. Freight transport on the other hand held up remarkably well over the same period.
3) I’m not sure what vehicles/km of expressway is supposed to suggest. The ratio might be higher or lower for each country depending on population density, political will to build basic infrastructure in the relative hinterlands, and degree of car ownership in urban areas. Also, why these particular countries?
4) While freight turnover on the railroads is markedly down from the peak, passenger turnover seems to have recovered to levels seen before the start of the COVID-19 pandemic.
5) Distance raw materials are transported on the Chinese railroads have dropped on average, even as the total volumes have increased. So what? Does it matter to the larger picture whether the railroad is transporting more stuff shorter distances or less stuff longer distances?
6) That dip in marginal passenger-traffic intensity, what do you think caused it? (Hint: it starts with “C” and rhymes with corvid). Is that a good indication of long-term overcapacity?
7) Same pattern as (6) above.
8) Ibid.
etc.
The author acts as if he’s identified a pattern where the total quantity of transportation is stagnant while the amount of infrastructure is expanding, but what he actually describes is the intensity of transportation remaining roughly constant, on average To draw an analogy, it’s like me going “I used to keep one chicken that laid an egg every day, back in the golden days. Now I keep two chicken that strong>each lay an egg every day, oh woe is me!”
(In reality, I could still be ahead if the second chicken only lays an egg every other day. But that’s getting into the reeds)
Like, there’s almost no argument in the article for real overcapacity? The closest he got was tolls not paying for the toll roads (I disagree with the implicit assumption that toll roads should necessarily be paid for through tolls alone), and the vast majority of air travel flows through a relatively small number of airports (the author may be shocked to learn that water is wet).

95,2026-09-29,"Demand for the latest iPhone 18 isn't that hot, likely because the iPhone 17 Pro Max is still a powerhouse despite its September 2025 release. Nonetheless, the latest iPhone 18 Pro Max will offer a slight spec boost, thanks to the newer, more efficient Apple A20 Pro chipset. Improved battery life is also a huge plus, while photographers are likely looking forward to the variable aperture update coming to the new iPhone's camera system. Is there an Android phone equivalent to the iPhone 18 Pro Max for those that aren't keen on joining the Apple ecosystem?
Not surprisingly, the well-reviewed Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra occupies the same space and is pretty close spec-wise. The physical size difference is negligible (Samsung is slightly bigger), and both phones come with 12 GB of RAM. The key difference is that Samsung offers a 16 GB RAM version. Apple bridges this gap by offering a 2 TB storage option, which is 1 TB more than Samsung. In terms of chipsets, the iPhone 18 Pro Max has a hexa-core processor while the S26 Ultra packs in an octa-core CPU. On paper, Samsung's octa-core 2x4.74 GHz Oryon V3 Phoenix L, boosted by a 6x3.62 GHz Oryon V3, should deliver better multi-core performance, while the iPhone should theoretically be better at single-core processing.
Perhaps the biggest difference between the two is in the camera department. The top-of-the-line Galaxy model may dwarf the new iPhone Pro in megapixels (200 on the main sensor, to be exact), but the iPhone 18 Pro Max has a physical variable-aperture camera that opens and closes. That could be a selling point for photography enthusiasts.
So? Which is better, the iPhone 18 Pro Max or the Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra? Neither, as it's only fair to say that the differences are negligible. Sure, you could point to the new iPhone 18 Pro Max having 500 extra mAh (5567 mAh) as proof it's better, but in all other areas, the race is so close that both phones will do everything you throw at them. Gaming, photography, multitasking, AI features – they can do it all. It's a bit like comparing two different flavors of ice cream. Whether they're worth it is a much better question, but it doesn't have a clear answer.
The pre-order price for the iPhone 18 Pro Max is $1,299, the same as the Samsung Galaxy S26 with 256 GB of storage and 12GB of RAM. If you've got the money and want one of the most powerful phones on the market, it's worth it. However, since both the regular Samsung Galaxy S26 and the now ""last-generation"" iPhone 17 Pro Max are still amazing devices and cost less, upgrading to the latest releases from both manufacturers technically isn't a big enough jump to justify the price.
You also haven't considered other manufacturers. For example, the latest Google Pixel 11 Pro isn't exactly a massive update from its predecessor, but it can provide similar performance to the newest iPhones and Samsungs – at $200 less. Overall, the spec improvements in 2026 weren't that big, and the real generational jump is more likely to happen around 2027. So, if your phone still works, it may be better to wait about a year to snag yourself a flagship that justifies both the hype and associated costs."
96,2026-09-29,"

FILE PHOTO: Clothes from fast-fashion brand Shein hang at their office in Sao Paulo, Brazil, December 15, 2025. REUTERS/Jorge Silva/File Photo
HONG KONG, Sept 29 : Shares of fast-fashion retailer Shein dropped more than 6 per cent on Tuesday after it reported a 67 per cent fall in quarterly profit, fuelling investor concerns over margin pressure and slowing growth. 
Jefferies analysts estimated that earnings landed more than 10 per cent below the low end of the range implied by Shein's prospectus. 
Adjusted net profit was $228 million for the second quarter and its margin was squeezed to just 2.1 per cent from 6.2 per cent last year as conflict in the Middle East pushed up jet fuel and freight costs for the retailer that sends cheap clothes by air to shoppers around the world. 
Since its September 1 stock market debut in Hong Kong to Monday's market close, Shein's shares have dropped 27.3 per cent from the offer price of HK$48.56 ($6.19). The stock was trading around HK$33.40 on Tuesday. 
Shein CEO and Chair Yangtian Xu said on Monday a key priority was increasing the amount of inventory in Europe and the company plans to push into higher-priced clothes that will boost its profitability. 
($1 = 7.8443 Hong Kong dollars)
 (Reporting By Anne Marie Roantree; Editing by Christian Schmollinger and Tom Hogue)
